NOTE ON THIS ROUND: the expert returned a verdict, not new theorems. It rules the little-o proposition UNDECIDED (neither it nor the reverse critical-scale lower bound is unconditionally provable now), states that current unconditional results cover only existence/odd multiplicity/size of a single primitive divisor (Granville 2012, Hong 2025) and give no average-dispersion or critical-concentration theorem for exact-rank prime multiplicities in the visible-divisor window, and notes that stronger prime-factor growth remains conjectural (Bugeaud-Luca-Mignotte-Siksek 2005). Crucially it RETRACTS the earlier claim that fibotomic entropy already exhausts the corresponding information, and withdraws the associated optimality assertion pending an information-axiom class and a realizable extremal model.

YOUR FIRST TASK is therefore an overclaim audit: find every place in this manuscript that asserts, or lets a reader infer, that fibotomic entropy exhausts the information or is optimal in that sense. For each, judge independently whether the manuscript actually proves it. Where it does not, restate it at the strength that is proved and record the gap explicitly as an open interface. Do not simply delete; state what is and is not established. Verify the three cited works say what is claimed before relying on them.

Then continue with the standard protocol below.

An external expert (ChatGPT 5.6 Pro) was given this paper's PDF and asked to produce deep, publication-worthy NEW results. Its reply is at artifacts/oracle_sprint_A7_r4.md (UTF-8, Chinese + LaTeX). Read it IN FULL.

YOUR PRIMARY DUTY IS VERIFICATION, NOT IMPLEMENTATION. Do not adopt anything on authority. Formal academic language. Do NOT git commit.

STEP 1 — VERIFY THE REASONING (not just the conclusions):
For EACH claimed result, check independently:
 (a) CORRECTNESS: reconstruct the argument yourself. Test the key identities/claims symbolically or numerically over a real parameter range. If the expert states a chain of reasoning, check every load-bearing step; a plausible-sounding step that is unproved counts as NOT verified. Actively search for a counterexample.
 (b) NOT-ALREADY-OURS: is it genuinely new relative to what THIS manuscript already proves (not a restatement/relabelling)?
 (c) NOT-ALREADY-PUBLISHED: query the arXiv API and standard sources. We may cite others' results but must never re-derive a published theorem as ours.
Assign each claim exactly one verdict: CORRECT-AND-NEW / UNVERIFIED (plausible but a step is unproved — say which) / WRONG (give the disproof or counterexample) / ALREADY-IN-PAPER / ALREADY-PUBLISHED (give the citation).

STEP 2 — ACT ON THE VERDICTS:
 - CORRECT-AND-NEW: prove rigorously and integrate into the manuscript with explicit hypotheses, no overclaim; extend the artifacts/ verification script to cover it and run it.
 - UNVERIFIED: do NOT integrate as a theorem. Either close the gap yourself and then integrate, or record it as an explicit open interface.
 - WRONG / ALREADY-*: do not integrate; state the disproof or citation plainly.
 Recompile (latexmk -pdfxe -interaction=nonstopmode -halt-on-error main.tex, or xelatex if that is the paper's engine).

STEP 3 — DESIGN THE NEXT ROUND:
Write the next-round follow-up to the file next_A7_r4.txt in this paper's directory. It must be in Chinese, formal academic register, and must do whichever applies:
 - CHALLENGE any WRONG claim by stating the counterexample and demanding a corrected statement;
 - PRESS any UNVERIFIED claim on exactly the unproved step, demanding a complete proof of that step;
 - If claims were ALREADY-IN-PAPER/PUBLISHED, say so and redirect the expert away from that ground;
 - Then pose the single sharpest genuinely-open question this paper now faces, demanding a deep publishable answer rather than incremental restatement.
End the file with: 不要重复之前内容.不要重复其他人已经发表公开的推理过程(定理、推论、猜想、命题及证明), 可以使用其他人的结论并引用.不要中间过程结论.请使用学术化语言表达, 禁止口语.

## Instruction Priority

Superpowers skills override default system prompt behavior, but **user instructions always take precedence**:

1. **User's explicit instructions** (CLAUDE.md, GEMINI.md, AGENTS.md, direct requests) â€” highest priority
2. **Superpowers skills** â€” override default system behavior where they conflict
3. **Default system prompt** â€” lowest priority

If CLAUDE.md, GEMINI.md, or AGENTS.md says "don't use TDD" and a skill says "always use TDD," follow the user's instructions. The user is in control.

## How to Access Skills

**In Claude Code:** Use the `Skill` tool. When you invoke a skill, its content is loaded and presented to youâ€”follow it directly. Never use the Read tool on skill files.

**In Copilot CLI:** Use the `skill` tool. Skills are auto-discovered from installed plugins. The `skill` tool works the same as Claude Code's `Skill` tool.

**In Gemini CLI:** Skills activate via the `activate_skill` tool. Gemini loads skill metadata at session start and activates the full content on demand.

**In other environments:** Check your platform's documentation for how skills are loaded.

## Platform Adaptation

Skills use Claude Code tool names. Non-CC platforms: see `references/copilot-tools.md` (Copilot CLI), `references/codex-tools.md` (Codex) for tool equivalents. Gemini CLI users get the tool mapping loaded automatically via GEMINI.md.

# Using Skills

## The Rule

**Invoke relevant or requested skills BEFORE any response or action.** Even a 1% chance a skill might apply means that you should invoke the skill to check. If an invoked skill turns out to be wrong for the situation, you don't need to use it.

```dot
digraph skill_flow {
    "User message received" [shape=doublecircle];
    "About to EnterPlanMode?" [shape=doublecircle];
    "Already brainstormed?" [shape=diamond];
    "Invoke brainstorming skill" [shape=box];
    "Might any skill apply?" [shape=diamond];
    "Invoke Skill tool" [shape=box];
    "Announce: 'Using [skill] to [purpose]'" [shape=box];
    "Has checklist?" [shape=diamond];
    "Create TodoWrite todo per item" [shape=box];
    "Follow skill exactly" [shape=box];
    "Respond (including clarifications)" [shape=doublecircle];

    "About to EnterPlanMode?" -> "Already brainstormed?";
    "Already brainstormed?" -> "Invoke brainstorming skill" [label="no"];
    "Already brainstormed?" -> "Might any skill apply?" [label="yes"];
    "Invoke brainstorming skill" -> "Might any skill apply?";

    "User message received" -> "Might any skill apply?";
    "Might any skill apply?" -> "Invoke Skill tool" [label="yes, even 1%"];
    "Might any skill apply?" -> "Respond (including clarifications)" [label="definitely not"];
    "Invoke Skill tool" -> "Announce: 'Using [skill] to [purpose]'";
    "Announce: 'Using [skill] to [purpose]'" -> "Has checklist?";
    "Has checklist?" -> "Create TodoWrite todo per item" [label="yes"];
    "Has checklist?" -> "Follow skill exactly" [label="no"];
    "Create TodoWrite todo per item" -> "Follow skill exactly";
}
```

## Red Flags

These thoughts mean STOPâ€”you're rationalizing:

| Thought | Reality |
|---------|---------|
| "This is just a simple question" | Questions are tasks. Check for skills. |
| "I need more context first" | Skill check comes BEFORE clarifying questions. |
| "Let me explore the codebase first" | Skills tell you HOW to explore. Check first. |
| "I can check git/files quickly" | Files lack conversation context. Check for skills. |
| "Let me gather information first" | Skills tell you HOW to gather information. |
| "This doesn't need a formal skill" | If a skill exists, use it. |
| "I remember this skill" | Skills evolve. Read current version. |
| "This doesn't count as a task" | Action = task. Check for skills. |
| "The skill is overkill" | Simple things become complex. Use it. |
| "I'll just do this one thing first" | Check BEFORE doing anything. |
| "This feels productive" | Undisciplined action wastes time. Skills prevent this. |
| "I know what that means" | Knowing the concept â‰  using the skill. Invoke it. |

## Skill Priority

When multiple skills could apply, use this order:

1. **Process skills first** (brainstorming, debugging) - these determine HOW to approach the task
2. **Implementation skills second** (frontend-design, mcp-builder) - these guide execution

"Let's build X" â†’ brainstorming first, then implementation skills.
"Fix this bug" â†’ debugging first, then domain-specific skills.

## Skill Types

**Rigid** (TDD, debugging): Follow exactly. Don't adapt away discipline.

**Flexible** (patterns): Adapt principles to context.

The skill itself tells you which.

## User Instructions

Instructions say WHAT, not HOW. "Add X" or "Fix Y" doesn't mean skip workflows.

# Writing Plans

## Overview

Write comprehensive implementation plans assuming the engineer has zero context for our codebase and questionable taste. Document everything they need to know: which files to touch for each task, code, testing, docs they might need to check, how to test it. Give them the whole plan as bite-sized tasks. DRY. YAGNI. TDD. Frequent commits.

Assume they are a skilled developer, but know almost nothing about our toolset or problem domain. Assume they don't know good test design very well.

**Announce at start:** "I'm using the writing-plans skill to create the implementation plan."

**Context:** This should be run in a dedicated worktree (created by brainstorming skill).

**Save plans to:** `docs/superpowers/plans/YYYY-MM-DD-<feature-name>.md`
- (User preferences for plan location override this default)

## Scope Check

If the spec covers multiple independent subsystems, it should have been broken into sub-project specs during brainstorming. If it wasn't, suggest breaking this into separate plans â€” one per subsystem. Each plan should produce working, testable software on its own.

## File Structure

Before defining tasks, map out which files will be created or modified and what each one is responsible for. This is where decomposition decisions get locked in.

- Design units with clear boundaries and well-defined interfaces. Each file should have one clear responsibility.
- You reason best about code you can hold in context at once, and your edits are more reliable when files are focused. Prefer smaller, focused files over large ones that do too much.
- Files that change together should live together. Split by responsibility, not by technical layer.
- In existing codebases, follow established patterns. If the codebase uses large files, don't unilaterally restructure - but if a file you're modifying has grown unwieldy, including a split in the plan is reasonable.

This structure informs the task decomposition. Each task should produce self-contained changes that make sense independently.

## Bite-Sized Task Granularity

**Each step is one action (2-5 minutes):**
- "Write the failing test" - step
- "Run it to make sure it fails" - step
- "Implement the minimal code to make the test pass" - step
- "Run the tests and make sure they pass" - step
- "Commit" - step

## Plan Document Header

**Every plan MUST start with this header:**

```markdown
# [Feature Name] Implementation Plan

> **For agentic workers:** REQUIRED SUB-SKILL: Use superpowers:subagent-driven-development (recommended) or superpowers:executing-plans to implement this plan task-by-task. Steps use checkbox (`- [ ]`) syntax for tracking.

**Goal:** [One sentence describing what this builds]

**Architecture:** [2-3 sentences about approach]

**Tech Stack:** [Key technologies/libraries]

---
```

## Task Structure

````markdown
### Task N: [Component Name]

**Files:**
- Create: `exact/path/to/file.py`
- Modify: `exact/path/to/existing.py:123-145`
- Test: `tests/exact/path/to/test.py`

- [ ] **Step 1: Write the failing test**

```python
def test_specific_behavior():
    result = function(input)
    assert result == expected
```

- [ ] **Step 2: Run test to verify it fails**

Run: `pytest tests/path/test.py::test_name -v`
Expected: FAIL with "function not defined"

- [ ] **Step 3: Write minimal implementation**

```python
def function(input):
    return expected
```

- [ ] **Step 4: Run test to verify it passes**

Run: `pytest tests/path/test.py::test_name -v`
Expected: PASS

- [ ] **Step 5: Commit**

```bash
git add tests/path/test.py src/path/file.py
git commit -m "feat: add specific feature"
```
````

## No Placeholders

Every step must contain the actual content an engineer needs. These are **plan failures** â€” never write them:
- "TBD", "TODO", "implement later", "fill in details"
- "Add appropriate error handling" / "add validation" / "handle edge cases"
- "Write tests for the above" (without actual test code)
- "Similar to Task N" (repeat the code â€” the engineer may be reading tasks out of order)
- Steps that describe what to do without showing how (code blocks required for code steps)
- References to types, functions, or methods not defined in any task

## Remember
- Exact file paths always
- Complete code in every step â€” if a step changes code, show the code
- Exact commands with expected output
- DRY, YAGNI, TDD, frequent commits

## Self-Review

After writing the complete plan, look at the spec with fresh eyes and check the plan against it. This is a checklist you run yourself â€” not a subagent dispatch.

**1. Spec coverage:** Skim each section/requirement in the spec. Can you point to a task that implements it? List any gaps.

**2. Placeholder scan:** Search your plan for red flags â€” any of the patterns from the "No Placeholders" section above. Fix them.

**3. Type consistency:** Do the types, method signatures, and property names you used in later tasks match what you defined in earlier tasks? A function called `clearLayers()` in Task 3 but `clearFullLayers()` in Task 7 is a bug.

If you find issues, fix them inline. No need to re-review â€” just fix and move on. If you find a spec requirement with no task, add the task.

## Execution Handoff

After saving the plan, offer execution choice:

**"Plan complete and saved to `docs/superpowers/plans/<filename>.md`. Two execution options:**

**1. Subagent-Driven (recommended)** - I dispatch a fresh subagent per task, review between tasks, fast iteration

**2. Inline Execution** - Execute tasks in this session using executing-plans, batch execution with checkpoints

**Which approach?"**

**If Subagent-Driven chosen:**
- **REQUIRED SUB-SKILL:** Use superpowers:subagent-driven-development
- Fresh subagent per task + two-stage review

**If Inline Execution chosen:**
- **REQUIRED SUB-SKILL:** Use superpowers:executing-plans
- Batch execution with checkpoints for review

env_map.tmp.txt:56:  \begin{example}\label{ex:n30} For $n=30$, $F_{30}=832040=2^3\cdot 5\cdot 11\cdot 31\cdot 61$. The witness covers are $\{31\}$, $\{4,5\}$, $\{2,11\}$, $\{4,61\}$, $\{11,61\}$, giving $\mathcal M_{30}=\{20,22,31,244,671\}$ and $A(30)=52$. With coordinates $(2,3,5)$ for $30=2\cdot3\cdot5$, these covers realize respectively the types $\Gamma_1$, $\Gamma_8$, $\Gamma_7$, $\Gamma_5$, and $\Gamma_4$. scope_contract.md:10:        Establish the theorem chain of 'Upper fibers and witness covers for the Fibonacci order-of-apparition map'. For each positive integer $n$, let $B_n$ denote the set of divisors of the Fibonacci number $F_n$ whose rank of apparition equals~$n$. We prove that $B_n$ is an upper set in the divisor lattice of $F_n$ obtained by removing the covering ideals associated with the maximal proper divisors of~$n$. Our main result classifies the minimal generators of~$B_n$: every minimal generator factors uniquely as a pairwise coprime product of atomic prime-power birth moduli forming a witness cover whose support spans the prime coordinates of~$n$. This yields the bound $(m)(n)$ for every minimal generator $m$ and a primitive-versus-ladder dichotomy for atomic factors. We give complete classifications for $(n) 3$ and show that both $B_n$ and its minimal-generator set admit unique factorizations into connected coordinate blocks, producing partition-lattice formulas for $A(n) = \#B_n$ and $\#M_n$. As consequ
scope_contract.md:17:        maximal-order estimate, the Sperner--Wigert comparison, and the
scope_contract.md:18:        sharp private-coordinate entropy bounds for $\log\#\mathcal M_n$ and

theorem_inventory.md:16:- `thm:minimal-generator-growth`: new headline arithmetic result. With `k=omega(n)` and `R(n)` the largest atomic rank-window multiplicity, it proves the sharp private-coordinate bounds `(2^floor(k/2)-1)^ceil(k/2) <= #M_n <= sum_{s<=k} binom(k,s)(2R(n))^s 2^{s(k-s)}` for `k>=3`. Hence `log #M_n` lies between `(log 2/4)k^2+O(1)` and `(log 2/4)k^2+k log R(n)+O(k)`. It gives the unconditional mean lower bound `(log 2/4+o(1))x(log log x)^2`, and exact normal and mean orders under explicit rank-window sparsity hypotheses. The earlier finite, maximal-order, and Sperner-Wigert bounds remain in force.

 3335: over all its possible full supports. Thus the atom assigned to $x$ has at most
 3336: $r2^{s-t}+1$ choices. Summing over $P$ and $t$ proves

 3358: \begin{theorem}[Blockwise criterion for connected dominance]
 3359: \label{thm:blockwise-connected-dominance}
 3360: Let $(n_j)$ be a sequence, put $V_j=\mathcal P(n_j)$ and
 3361: $k_j=\omega(n_j)\to\infty$, and for nonempty $C\subseteq V_j$ put
 3362: \[
 3363:  (n_j)_C:=\prod_{\ell\in C}\ell^{\nu_\ell(n_j)}.
 3364: \]
 3365: For a nontrivial partition $\Pi\in\mathsf{Part}(V_j)$ define
 3366: \[
 3367:  \Delta(\Pi):=\sum_{\substack{C,D\in\Pi\\C<D}}|C||D|
 3368:  =\frac{k_j^2-\sum_{C\in\Pi}|C|^2}{2}.
 3369: \]
 3370: Suppose
 3371: \begin{equation}\label{eq:blockwise-window-condition}
 3372:  \varepsilon_j:=
 3373:  \max_{\substack{\Pi\in\mathsf{Part}(V_j)\\|\Pi|\ge2}}
 3374:  \frac{\sum_{C\in\Pi}|C|\log R((n_j)_C)}{\Delta(\Pi)}
 3375:  \longrightarrow0\qquad\text{\rm(BW)}.
 3376: \end{equation}
 3377: Then
 3378: \begin{equation}\label{eq:connected-dominance-conclusion}
 3379:  \frac{\#(\mathcal M_{n_j}\setminus\mathcal M_{n_j}^{\rm conn})}
 3380:  {\#\mathcal M_{n_j}^{\rm conn}}
 3381:  \le\exp\left[-\left(\frac{\log2}{2}-o(1)\right)k_j\right]
 3382:  =o(1).
 3383: \end{equation}
 3384: A sufficient condition for \eqref{eq:blockwise-window-condition} is
 3385: \begin{equation}\label{eq:blockwise-window-sufficient-condition}
 3386:  \max_{\varnothing\ne C\subsetneq V_j}
 3387:  \frac{\log R((n_j)_C)}{k_j-|C|}\longrightarrow0
 3388:  \qquad\text{\rm(BW$'$)}.
 3389: \end{equation}
 3390: In particular, \eqref{eq:connected-dominance-conclusion} holds if
 3391: $R((n_j)_C)=1$ for every nonempty proper block $C$ and all sufficiently
 3392: large $j$.
 3393: \end{theorem}
 3394: 
 3395: \begin{proof}
 3396: The unique connected-block factorization in
 3397: Theorem~\ref{thm:mingenerator-connected-factorization} gives
 3398: \[
 3399:  \#(\mathcal M_{n_j}\setminus\mathcal M_{n_j}^{\rm conn})
 3400:  =\sum_{\substack{\Pi\in\mathsf{Part}(V_j)\\|\Pi|\ge2}}
 3401:    \prod_{C\in\Pi}M^{\rm conn}((n_j)_C).
 3402: \]
 3403: Apply Lemma~\ref{lem:refined-private-coordinate-upper-bound} to each block.
 3404: The theta asymptotic \eqref{eq:minimal-cover-theta} gives uniform two-sided
 3405: bounds
 3406: \[
 3407:  C_s\asymp(s+1)^{-1/2}2^{s^2/4+s}.
 3408: \]
 3409: Consequently there is an absolute constant $K_1$ such that every partition
 3410: $\Pi$ with block sizes $s_C=|C|$ contributes, after division by $C_{k_j}$,
 3411: at most
 3412: \begin{equation}\label{eq:partition-contribution-bound}
 3413:  K_1^{|\Pi|}\sqrt{k_j}\,
 3414:  \exp\left\{-\frac{\log2}{2}\Delta(\Pi)
 3415:  +\sum_{C\in\Pi}|C|\log R((n_j)_C)\right\}.
 3416: \end{equation}
 3417: Under \textup{(BW)}, this is
 3418: \[
 3419:  K_1^{|\Pi|}\sqrt{k_j}\,
 3420:  \exp\left\{-\left(\frac{\log2}{2}-o(1)\right)
 3421:  \Delta(\Pi)\right\},
 3422: \]
 3423: where the $o(1)$ is uniform over all nontrivial partitions.
 3424: 
 3425: If every block has size at most $k_j/2$, then
 3426: $\Delta(\Pi)\ge k_j^2/4$. There are at most $k_j^{k_j}$ set partitions,
 3427: so the total of these terms is $\exp(-\Omega(k_j^2))$. Otherwise there is a
 3428: unique largest block of size $k_j-q>k_j/2$. Then
 3429: $\Delta(\Pi)\ge q(k_j-q)$, and the remaining $q$ vertices can be selected and
 3430: partitioned in at most
 3431: \[
 3432:  \binom{k_j}{q}\mathsf B_q\le(ek_j)^q
 3433: \]
 3434: ways. Summing \eqref{eq:partition-contribution-bound} over
 3435: $1\le q<k_j/2$ therefore gives
 3436: \[
 3437:  \exp\left[-\left(\frac{\log2}{2}-o(1)\right)k_j\right]C_{k_j}.
 3438: \]
 3439: Corollary~\ref{cor:theta-refined-connected-entropy} gives
 3440: $M^{\rm conn}(n_j)\ge(1-o(1))C_{k_j}$, proving
 3441: \eqref{eq:connected-dominance-conclusion}.
 3442: 
 3443: Finally, if the maximum in \textup{(BW$'$)} is $\eta_j$, then every block of
 3444: a nontrivial partition is proper and
 3445: \[
 3446:  \sum_{C\in\Pi}|C|\log R((n_j)_C)
 3447:  \le\eta_j\sum_{C\in\Pi}|C|(k_j-|C|)
 3448:  =2\eta_j\Delta(\Pi).
 3449: \]
 3450: Thus \textup{(BW$'$)} implies \textup{(BW)}, and the last assertion is the
 3451: case $\eta_j=0$.
 3452: \end{proof}

 3455: \section{Numerical evidence}\label{sec:numerical}
 3456: %% ====================================================================

 3458: Table~\ref{tab:birth-layer-data-generated} displays the birth layer cardinality $A(n)$
 3459: and the number of minimal generators $\#\mathcal M_n$ for $2\le n\le 30$,
 3460: computed by exhaustive enumeration. The table is illustrative only: the
 3461: unconditional inequalities in Theorem~\ref{thm:minimal-generator-growth} do
 3462: not rely on this finite sample. Several features visible in the
 3463: data agree with the structural results of the preceding sections.
 3464: First, $\#\mathcal M_n=1$ whenever $\omega(n)=1$ and
 3465: $n\notin\{19,25,27\}$; the exceptions arise from composite Fibonacci numbers
 3466: with multiple atomic families at the same prime-power index.
 3467: Second, the jump at $n=12$ ($A(12)=10$, $\#\mathcal M_{12}=3$) and at $n=24$
 3468: ($A(24)=55$, $\#\mathcal M_{24}=4$) reflects the interaction of the
 3469: exceptional prime $2$ with the layers having no exact-rank prime at indices
 3470: $6$ and $12$.
 3471: Third, the first nonempty three-coordinate layer occurs at $n=30$ and has
 3472: $\#\mathcal M_{30}=5$; the displayed witness covers realize exactly the
 3473: types $\Gamma_1$, $\Gamma_4$, $\Gamma_5$, $\Gamma_7$, and $\Gamma_8$.
 3474: The remaining admissible shapes $\Gamma_3$, $\Gamma_6$, and $\Gamma_9$ are
 3475: not realized at $n=30$. This exact five-type assertion, including the three
 3476: nonrealization proofs, is Proposition~\ref{prop:n30-five-type-classification};
 3477: it is not inferred from the table.
 3478: 
 3479: The table was produced by the finite divisor/rank computation in the next
 3480: proposition, applied exhaustively for $2\le n\le30$.  The manuscript source
 3481: includes the short script that performs this enumeration and the generated
 3482: table file input below; these files are supplementary to the mathematical
 3483: criterion rather than part of the theorem stream. For the classical
 3484: factorization literature, see the tables of Brillhart--Montgomery--Silverman
 3485: \cite{BrillhartMontgomerySilverman1988Tables}.
 3486: 
 3487: \begin{proposition}[Finite divisor/rank verification with supplied factorization]
 3488: \label{prop:effective-birth-layer-computation}
 3489: Let $n\ge2$ and suppose the integer factorization of $F_n$ is known. Then
 3490: $B_n$, $A(n)$, and $\mathcal M_n$ are recovered exactly by the following finite
 3491: divisor/rank test.
 3492: \begin{enumerate}[label=(\alph*)]
 3493: \item Enumerate the divisor set $D_n=\operatorname{Div}(F_n)$ from the
 3494:  factorization of $F_n$.
 3495: \item Enumerate the divisor set $\operatorname{Div}(n)$. For each
 3496:  $q\in D_n$, compute
 3497:  \[
 3498:  \alpha_n(q):=\min\{d\mid n:q\mid F_d\}.
 3499:  \]
 3500: \item Set $B_n=\{q\in D_n:q\ge2,\ \alpha_n(q)=n\}$ and
 3501:  $A(n)=\#B_n$.
 3502: \item Sort $B_n$ by divisibility-compatible order, for instance by size, and
 3503:  retain exactly those $m\in B_n$ for which no previously retained element
 3504:  divides $m$; the retained set is $\mathcal M_n$.
 3505: \end{enumerate}
 3506: \end{proposition}
 3507: 
 3508: \begin{proof}
 3509: Since every $q\in D_n$ divides $F_n$, Proposition~\ref{prop:rank-basics}
 3510: shows that $q\mid F_d$ for some $d$ if and only if $\alpha(q)\mid d$. In
 3511: particular $q\mid F_n$ implies $\alpha(q)\mid n$, so the least positive index
 3512: at which $q$ appears is exactly
 3513: \[
 3514: \alpha(q)=\min\{d\mid n:q\mid F_d\}=\alpha_n(q).
 3515: \]
 3516: Hence the test in step (c) gives precisely the definition
 3517: $B_n=\{q\in D_n:\alpha(q)=n\}$, with the harmless exclusion of $q=1$ already
 3518: consistent with the convention $\alpha(1)=1$ and $n\ge2$. Counting this set
 3519: gives $A(n)$.
 3520: 
 3521: It remains to justify step (d). The set $B_n$ is finite. If its elements are
 3522: processed in increasing size, then every proper divisor of the current element
 3523: has already been processed. We claim inductively that every discarded element
 3524: has a retained minimal divisor. The claim is vacuous before the first element.
 3525: If the current element is discarded, it has a previously retained divisor by
 3526: the algorithm, so the claim holds for it; if it is retained, it is its own
 3527: retained divisor. Thus every earlier divisor, retained or discarded, has a
 3528: retained divisor below it. Consequently the current element is retained if and
 3529: only if no smaller element of $B_n$ divides it, which is exactly minimality in
 3530: the divisibility poset. The retained set is therefore $\mathcal M_n$.
 3531: \end{proof}
 3532: 
 3533: \begin{remark}[Reproducibility scope]
 3534: The preceding proposition is used only to document the finite verification
 3535: interface for the table, not as a complexity theorem.  After the factorization
 3536: of $F_n$ has been supplied, the displayed loops involve only the finite divisor
 3537: sets of $F_n$ and $n$, ordinary divisibility tests $q\mid F_d$, and divisor
 3538: comparisons inside $B_n$.  The manuscript makes no bit-complexity assertion and
 3539: does not include the cost of factoring $F_n$ in the numerical evidence.
 3540: \end{remark}
 3541: 
 3542: \paragraph{Table generation.}
 3543: The reproducibility files
 3544: \[
 3545: \text{\texttt{artifacts/compute\_birth\_layer\_table.py}},\qquad
 3546: \text{\texttt{artifacts/tab\_birth\_layer\_data.tex}}
 3547: \]
 3548: are included with the manuscript source. Running the command in
 3549: \texttt{artifacts/REPRODUCE.md}
 3550: \[
 3551: \texttt{python artifacts/compute\_birth\_layer\_table.py}
 3552: \]
 3553: regenerates the table input from the divisor/rank method of
 3554: Proposition~\ref{prop:effective-birth-layer-computation}.  The printed table is
 3555: therefore an exhaustive enumeration of $A(n)$ and $\#\mathcal M_n$ for
 3556: $2\le n\le30$ using only the finite tests proved above; the displayed
 3557: factorizations of the small integers $F_n$ are produced by the script's exact
 3558: integer trial-division routine. Its complete console output is supplied as
 3559: \path{artifacts/birth_layer_table_output.txt}.
 3560: \input{generated/tab_birth_layer_data.tex}
 3561: 
 3562: \paragraph{Finite verification.}
 3563: The supplementary script
 3564: \texttt{artifacts/verify\_finite\_claims.py} independently compares the actual
 3565: sets obtained by direct divisor/rank enumeration with the upper-fiber and
 3566: witness constructions through $n=60$. It
 3567: then uses the latter formula through $n=210$: inclusion--exclusion computes
 3568: \[
 3569:  A(n)=\sum_{S\subseteq\mathcal P(n)}(-1)^{|S|}
 3570:  \tau\!\left(F_{n/\prod_{p\in S}p}\right),
 3571: \]
 3572: and the minimal threshold covers of the conditions
 3573: $q\nmid F_{n/p}$ compute $\mathcal M_n$.  The script checks
 3574: \eqref{eq:finite-minimal-generator-bound}, the simplified bound
 3575: $\#\mathcal M_n\le n^{\omega(n)}$, the odd Bell lower bound, and both finite
 3576: private-cover bounds in \eqref{eq:private-cover-finite-bounds}. It also
 3577: computes $R(n)$, checks the pointwise deaggregation bounds
 3578: \eqref{eq:prime-window-versus-R} and
 3579: \eqref{eq:pointwise-window-deaggregation}, verifies the exact-rank partition
 3580: and \eqref{eq:squarefree-blms-pigeonhole} on every squarefree layer, checks
 3581: the refined upper bound \eqref{eq:refined-private-coordinate-upper-bound},
 3582: checks the fibotomic identities \eqref{eq:fibotomic-binet-error} and
 3583: \eqref{eq:exact-rank-radical-divides-fibotomic}, the entropy inequality
 3584: \eqref{eq:fibotomic-rank-entropy}, the exact-rank congruence candidate bound,
 3585: and the available cases of \eqref{eq:jarden-exact-rank-consequence}, and
 3586: records the running ratio against the support-entropy scale
 3587: $(\log2)\omega(n)^2/4$. For the rank-pure results, it directly enumerates all
 3588: minimal covers for $k\le4$, checks the Hearne--Wagner and connected-component
 3589: counts, verifies the M\"obius exact-rank-prime formula on every support, and
 3590: tests every canonical rank-pure product against the independently computed
 3591: $\mathcal M_n$. This covers all $208$ layers $3\le n\le210$, including exact
 3592: realization of every minimal cover on all $104$ odd layers. It reports $59/59$
 3593: set equalities for both original comparisons and no finite-bound or rank-pure
 3594: failures. The exact output is
 3595: \path{artifacts/finite_verification.txt}. The exact factorizations used for
 3596: $2\le n\le210$ are recorded in
 3597: \path{artifacts/fibonacci_factorizations_2_210.tsv}; the verifier checks
 3598: every row by multiplication. The Python and SymPy versions, commands, file
 3599: inventory, and verification scope are in \path{artifacts/REPRODUCE.md}, and
 3600: \path{artifacts/SHA256SUMS} authenticates the supplied files. These finite
 3601: checks do not provide distributional control of $R(n)$.
 3602: In particular, they do not prove the asymptotic hypotheses \textup{(H2)} or
 3603: \textup{(BW)}.
 3604: 
 3605: %% ====================================================================
 3606: \section{Further questions}\label{sec:questions}
 3607: %% ====================================================================
 3608: 
 3609: The preceding sections complete the divisor-lattice theorem chain for this
 3610: article.  We record only two boundary markers; neither is used as evidence for
 3611: the results proved above.
 3612: \begin{enumerate}[label=(\arabic*)]
 3613: \item Classify connected support kernels of size at least four. By
 3614: Corollary~\ref{cor:four-support-reduction}, the one-, two-, and
 3615: three-coordinate classifications already determine every disconnected
 3616: four-coordinate pattern. Corollary~\ref{cor:theta-refined-connected-entropy}
 3617: shows that connected kernels already realize the complete known lower support
 3618: entropy, so a full connected high-support classification is not a low-entropy
 3619: residual problem. It would be a separate project.
 3620: \item Prove or disprove either rank-window sparsity conjecture. For the mean
 3621: hypothesis, Theorem~\ref{thm:mean-rank-window-deaggregation} reduces the
 3622: problem to the visible single-rank maximum
 3623: \eqref{eq:visible-single-rank-interface}; Proposition
 3624: \ref{prop:blms-obstructs-mean-window} also shows that a published conjecture
 3625: predicts failure rather than sparsity. As explained in
 3626: Remark~\ref{rem:exact-average-open-interface}, the remaining question is an
 3627: upper distribution problem for exact-rank prime multiplicities.
 3628: The paper derives conditional normal and mean orders, but does not
 3629: claim that either conjecture follows from presently known primitive-divisor
 3630: theorems, RH, or ERH.
 3631: \end{enumerate}
 3632: 
 3633: 
 3634: \appendix
 3635: 
 3636: \section{Finite verification for the three-coordinate table}
 3637: \label{app:three-coordinate-checklist}
 3638: 
 3639: \begin{proposition}[Reader's checklist for the three-coordinate table]
 3640: \label{prop:three-coordinate-table-checklist}
 3641: Assume $\mathcal P(n)=\{\ell_1,
 3642: \ell_2,\ell_3\}$.  The following finite procedure returns exactly
 3643: $\mathcal M_n$.
 3644: \begin{enumerate}[label=(\roman*)]
 3645: \item For every nonempty $J\subseteq[3]$, compute the exact-rank window
 3646: $\mathcal R_J^\circ(n)$ and the rank classes
 3647: $\Pi_\alpha(d)$ for $d\in\mathcal R_J^\circ(n)$ by
 3648: Lemma~\ref{lem:primitive-prime-mobius-count}. This gives every prime
 3649: slot $P_J(n)$.
 3650: \item For each coordinate $i$, compute the single possible ladder candidate
 3651: $\mathcal L_i^*(n)$ from

 succeeded in 324ms:
 2622: 
 2623: \begin{theorem}[Rank-pure universality]\label{thm:rank-pure-universality}
 2624: Let $n\ge3$, put $V=\mathcal P(n)$, and, for every nonempty $S\subseteq V$,
 2625: define
 2626: \[
 2627:  n_S:=\prod_{\ell\in S}\ell^{\nu_\ell(n)},\qquad
 2628:  a_n(S):=\#\Pi_\alpha(n_S),
 2629: \]
 2630: and
 2631: \[
 2632:  \mathcal E_n:=\{S\subseteq V:S\ne\varnothing,\ n_S\in\{2,6,12\}\}.
 2633: \]
 2634: Then $\#\mathcal E_n\le2$, and every member of $\mathcal E_n$ has cardinality
 2635: at most two. Define the rank-pure prime sector
 2636: \[
 2637:  \mathcal R_n^{\rm rp}:=
 2638:  \left\{
 2639:  \prod_{S\in\mathcal C}p_S:
 2640:  \begin{array}{l}
 2641:   \mathcal C\in\operatorname{MinCov}(V),\quad
 2642:   \mathcal C\cap\mathcal E_n=\varnothing,\\
 2643:   p_S\in\Pi_\alpha(n_S)\quad(S\in\mathcal C)
 2644:  \end{array}
 2645:  \right\}.
 2646: \]
 2647: Then $\mathcal R_n^{\rm rp}\subseteq\mathcal M_n$, the displayed representation is
 2648: unique, and
 2649: \begin{equation}\label{eq:rank-pure-weighted-sector}
 2650:  \#\mathcal R_n^{\rm rp}=
 2651:  \sum_{\substack{\mathcal C\in\operatorname{MinCov}(V)\\
 2652:                    \mathcal C\cap\mathcal E_n=\varnothing}}
 2653:  \prod_{S\in\mathcal C}a_n(S),
 2654:  \qquad
 2655:  a_n(S)=\sum_{d\mid n_S}\mu(n_S/d)\,\omega(F_d).
 2656: \end{equation}
 2657: Moreover, the support hypergraph of the product attached to $\mathcal C$ has
 2658: edge set exactly $\mathcal C$. In particular, connected minimal covers map to
 2659: $\mathcal M_n^{\rm conn}$, with their complete hypergraph structure preserved.
 2660: \end{theorem}
 2661: 
 2662: \begin{proof}
 2663: The map $S\mapsto n_S$ is injective. If $n_S=2$, then
 2664: $S=\{2\}$ and $\nu_2(n)=1$. If $n_S=6$, then
 2665: $S=\{2,3\}$ and $\nu_2(n)=\nu_3(n)=1$; if $n_S=12$, then
 2666: $S=\{2,3\}$, $\nu_2(n)=2$, and $\nu_3(n)=1$. Thus the ranks $6$ and $12$
 2667: cannot both occur, while rank $2$ can coexist only with rank $6$. This proves
 2668: the assertions about $\mathcal E_n$.
 2669: 
 2670: Fix $\mathcal C\in\operatorname{MinCov}(V)$ avoiding $\mathcal E_n$. For
 2671: every $S\in\mathcal C$, the integer $n_S$ is at least $3$ and is not $6$ or
 2672: $12$. Theorem~\ref{thm:primitive-divisors} therefore gives a prime
 2673: $p_S\in\Pi_\alpha(n_S)$. Distinct supports have distinct ranks, so the primes
 2674: $p_S$ are distinct. Since $p_S$ is a prime atom and $d^-(p_S)=1$,
 2675: \[
 2676:  E_n(p_S)=T_n(p_S)=S.
 2677: \]
 2678: Consequently the coverage and private-coordinate conditions defining
 2679: $\operatorname{MinCov}(V)$ are exactly the witness-cover conditions for
 2680: $\{p_S:S\in\mathcal C\}$. Theorem~\ref{thm:witness-classification} places
 2681: $\prod_{S\in\mathcal C}p_S$ in $\mathcal M_n$.
 2682: 
 2683: Unique factorization recovers every selected prime from the product; its rank
 2684: $\alpha(p_S)=n_S$ then recovers $S$. Hence both the cover and all prime choices
 2685: are recovered, proving injectivity and the weighted count in
 2686: \eqref{eq:rank-pure-weighted-sector}. The formula for $a_n(S)$ is
 2687: Lemma~\ref{lem:primitive-prime-mobius-count}. Finally,
 2688: \[
 2689:  \operatorname{supp}_n(\alpha(p_S))=
 2690:  \operatorname{supp}_n(n_S)=S,
 2691: \]
 2692: so Definition~\ref{def:support-hypergraph} gives precisely the edge set
 2693: $\mathcal C$ and therefore preserves connected components.
 2694: \end{proof}
 2695: 
 2696: \begin{corollary}[Odd-layer cover universality]
 2697: \label{cor:odd-rank-pure-universality}
 2698: Let $n\ge3$ be odd and put $k=\omega(n)$. Then $\mathcal E_n=\varnothing$.
 2699: After fixing one prime $\pi_S\in\Pi_\alpha(n_S)$ for every nonempty
 2700: $S\subseteq\mathcal P(n)$, the map
 2701: \[
 2702:  \mathcal C\longmapsto\prod_{S\in\mathcal C}\pi_S
 2703: \]
 2704: embeds $\operatorname{MinCov}(\mathcal P(n))$ into $\mathcal M_n$ and
 2705: preserves the support hypergraph. Hence, if $C_k$ denotes the number of minimal
 2706: covers of a labelled $k$-set, then
 2707: \begin{equation}\label{eq:odd-minimal-cover-bound}
 2708:  \#\mathcal M_n\ge C_k,
 2709:  \qquad
 2710:  \widthop(B_n)\ge C_k.
 2711: \end{equation}
 2712: Through Royle's correspondence
 2713: \cite{Royle2000SetCoversSplitGraphs}, every isomorphism type of split graph on
 2714: $k$ vertices consequently has a rank-pure Fibonacci realization on the prime
 2715: coordinates of $n$.
 2716: \end{corollary}
 2717: 
 2718: \begin{proof}
 2719: Every nonempty $n_S$ is odd and at least $3$, so no support is exceptional.
 2720: Theorem~\ref{thm:rank-pure-universality} gives the embedding and support
 2721: preservation. Minimal generators form an antichain, which proves the width
 2722: bound. Royle's theorem is a correspondence between isomorphism types of split
 2723: graphs and minimal covers; choosing a labelling of a representative cover and
 2724: then applying the embedding gives the final assertion.
 2725: \end{proof}
 2726: 
 2727: We next fix the classical enumeration input, without claiming it as a result of
 2728: this paper. Let $C_{k,s}$ count the minimal covers of a labelled $k$-set having
 2729: $s$ members, put $C_k=\sum_s C_{k,s}$, and put
 2730: \[
 2731:  b_k:=\sum_{s=0}^k\binom{k}{s}2^{s(k-s)}.
 2732: \]
 2733: Hearne and Wagner \cite{HearneWagner1973MinimalCovers} proved
 2734: \begin{equation}\label{eq:hearne-wagner}
 2735:  C_{k,s}=\sum_{j=s}^k\binom{k}{j}
 2736:  \genfrac{\{}{\}}{0pt}{}{j}{s}
 2737:  (2^s-s-1)^{k-j}.
 2738: \end{equation}
 2739: The central-sum asymptotic occurring in the labelled split-graph enumeration of
 2740: Bender--Richmond--Wormald \cite{BenderRichmondWormald1985ChordalSplit}, in the
 2741: form recorded also by Troyka \cite[Section~3]{Troyka2019SplitGraphs}, gives,
 2742: with
 2743: \[
 2744:  \vartheta_0:=\sum_{r\in\mathbb Z}2^{-r^2},\qquad
 2745:  \vartheta_1:=\sum_{r\in\mathbb Z}2^{-(r+1/2)^2},\qquad
 2746:  \varepsilon\equiv k\pmod2,
 2747: \]
 2748: the published asymptotic
 2749: \begin{equation}\label{eq:minimal-cover-theta}
 2750:  C_k\sim b_k\sim
 2751:  \vartheta_\varepsilon\binom{k}{\lfloor k/2\rfloor}2^{k^2/4}.
 2752: \end{equation}
 2753: In particular, $b_{k-1}/b_k=O(2^{-k/2})$.
 2754: 
 2755: \begin{lemma}[Connected minimal-cover concentration]
 2756: \label{lem:connected-minimal-cover-concentration}
 2757: If $C_k^{\rm conn}$ denotes the number of connected minimal covers, then
 2758: \begin{equation}\label{eq:connected-minimal-cover-asymptotic}
 2759:  C_k-C_k^{\rm conn}=O(kC_{k-1})=o(C_k).
 2760: \end{equation}
 2761: \end{lemma}
 2762: 
 2763: \begin{proof}
 2764: Connected components of a minimal cover are themselves minimal covers, so the
 2765: labelled exponential formula gives
 2766: \[
 2767:  C_k=\sum_{j=1}^k\binom{k-1}{j-1}C_j^{\rm conn}C_{k-j}.
 2768: \]
 2769: The terms $j=1$ and $j=k-1$ contribute $O(kC_{k-1})$. Choosing the least
 2770: private element in each cover member gives $C_j\le b_j\le2^j2^{j^2/4}$.
 2771: For the remaining terms, use this bound and the central lower bound for
 2772: $C_{k-1}$ furnished by the $s=\lfloor(k-1)/2\rfloor$ term of
 2773: \eqref{eq:hearne-wagner}. After division by $kC_{k-1}$, the term indexed by
 2774: $j$ is
 2775: \[
 2776:  \ll \binom{k}{j}
 2777:  2^{-(j-1)(k-j-1)/2}\qquad(2\le j\le k-2).
 2778: \]
 2779: The sum of these bounds is bounded (and in fact tends to zero), by symmetry
 2780: and separation into $2\le j\le k/2$ and its reflection. This proves the first
 2781: estimate in \eqref{eq:connected-minimal-cover-asymptotic}; the second equality
 2782: follows from \eqref{eq:minimal-cover-theta}.
 2783: \end{proof}
 2784: 
 2785: \begin{corollary}[Theta-refined connected support entropy]
 2786: \label{cor:theta-refined-connected-entropy}
 2787: Uniformly along every sequence of integers for which $k=\omega(n)\to\infty$,
 2788: \begin{equation}\label{eq:theta-connected-lower-bound}
 2789:  \#\mathcal M_n^{\rm conn}\ge
 2790:  (1-o(1))\,
 2791:  \vartheta_\varepsilon\binom{k}{\lfloor k/2\rfloor}2^{k^2/4},
 2792:  \qquad \varepsilon\equiv k\pmod2.
 2793: \end{equation}
 2794: Consequently
 2795: \begin{equation}\label{eq:theta-connected-log-lower-bound}
 2796:  \log\#\mathcal M_n^{\rm conn}\ge
 2797:  \frac{\log2}{4}k^2+k\log2-\frac12\log k
 2798:  +\log\vartheta_\varepsilon+\frac12\log\frac2\pi+o(1).
 2799: \end{equation}
 2800: The same lower bounds hold with $\mathcal M_n$ in place of
 2801: $\mathcal M_n^{\rm conn}$.
 2802: \end{corollary}
 2803: 
 2804: \begin{proof}
 2805: Fix an exceptional support $S_0\in\mathcal E_n$. If a minimal cover contains
 2806: $S_0$, choose a canonical private vertex $x\in S_0$ of that member. Every
 2807: other member avoids $x$ and is recovered from its canonical private vertex and
 2808: an arbitrary subset of the remaining nonprivate vertices. The number of such
 2809: encodings is at most $b_{k-1}$ for each choice of $x$. Therefore the number of
 2810: minimal covers meeting $\mathcal E_n$ is at most
 2811: \[
 2812:  \sum_{S_0\in\mathcal E_n}|S_0|b_{k-1}\le4b_{k-1}=o(C_k).
 2813: \]
 2814: Lemma~\ref{lem:connected-minimal-cover-concentration} also shows that only
 2815: $o(C_k)$ minimal covers are disconnected. Thus $(1-o(1))C_k$ covers are simultaneously
 2816: connected and disjoint from $\mathcal E_n$. Theorem
 2817: \ref{thm:rank-pure-universality} embeds them in
 2818: $\mathcal M_n^{\rm conn}$, proving \eqref{eq:theta-connected-lower-bound}.
 2819: Taking logarithms and using
 2820: \[
 2821:  \log\binom{k}{\lfloor k/2\rfloor}
 2822:  =k\log2-\frac12\log k+\frac12\log\frac2\pi+o(1)
 2823: \]
 2824: gives \eqref{eq:theta-connected-log-lower-bound}.
 2825: \end{proof}
 2826: 
 2827: \begin{corollary}[Discrete local limit in a fixed-prime rank-pure sector]
 2828: \label{cor:rank-pure-local-limit}
 2829: Let $(n_j)$ be any sequence with $k_j=\omega(n_j)\to\infty$. For every
 2830: $j$ and every nonexceptional nonempty support
 2831: $S\subseteq\mathcal P(n_j)$, fix one prime
 2832: $\pi_{j,S}\in\Pi_\alpha((n_j)_S)$. Let $\mathcal Q_{n_j}^{\rm rp}$ consist of the
 2833: products $\prod_{S\in\mathcal C}\pi_{j,S}$ over all
 2834: $\mathcal C\in\operatorname{MinCov}(\mathcal P(n_j))$ disjoint from
 2835: $\mathcal E_{n_j}$, and choose $m$ uniformly from $\mathcal Q_{n_j}^{\rm rp}$. Put
 2836: $W_j=\omega(m)$. Along the subsequence $k_j=2q_j$, for every fixed
 2837: $d\in\mathbb Z$,
 2838: \begin{equation}\label{eq:even-rank-pure-local-limit}
 2839:  \Pr(W_j=q_j+d)\longrightarrow\frac{2^{-d^2}}{\vartheta_0}.
 2840: \end{equation}
 2841: Along the subsequence $k_j=2q_j+1$,
 2842: \begin{equation}\label{eq:odd-rank-pure-local-limit}
 2843:  \Pr(W_j=q_j+d)\longrightarrow
 2844:  \frac{2^{-(d-1/2)^2}}{\vartheta_1}.
 2845: \end{equation}
 2846: In particular, $W_j=k_j/2+O_{\mathbb P}(1)$.
 2847: \end{corollary}
 2848: 
 2849: \begin{proof}
 2850: Unique factorization and distinct support ranks show that $W_j$ is the number
 2851: of members of the corresponding cover. By the proof of
 2852: Corollary~\ref{cor:theta-refined-connected-entropy}, deleting covers meeting
 2853: $\mathcal E_{n_j}$ changes the uniform distribution in total variation by
 2854: $o(1)$. For $s=\lfloor k/2\rfloor+d$ with fixed $d$, the $j=s$ term of
 2855: \eqref{eq:hearne-wagner} and the canonical-private-element encoding give
 2856: \[
 2857:  \binom{k}{s}(2^s-s-1)^{k-s}
 2858:  \le C_{k,s}\le \binom{k}{s}2^{s(k-s)},
 2859:  \qquad
 2860:  C_{k,s}\sim\binom{k}{s}2^{s(k-s)}.
 2861: \]
 2862: Dividing this by \eqref{eq:minimal-cover-theta} gives
 2863: \eqref{eq:even-rank-pure-local-limit} and
 2864: \eqref{eq:odd-rank-pure-local-limit}. The quadratic limiting tails are
 2865: summable, which is equivalent to tightness on the integer scale.
 2866: \end{proof}
 2867: 
 2868: \begin{remark}\label{rem:connected-lower-not-dominance}
 2869: Corollary~\ref{cor:theta-refined-connected-entropy} proves that connected
 2870: minimal generators alone realize the full currently known lower support
 2871: entropy, including its linear and logarithmic corrections. It does not compare
 2872: the total connected and disconnected parts of $\mathcal M_n$. Such a comparison
 2873: would require upper control of the arithmetic multiplicities in $R(n)$ and is
 2874: not presently available.
 2875: \end{remark}
 2876: 
 2877: \begin{theorem}[Combinatorial support entropy]
 2878: \label{thm:minimal-generator-growth}
 2879: For every integer $n\ge3$, put $k=\omega(n)$ and
 2880: $L(n)=\log\#\mathcal M_n$. Then $\mathcal M_n$ is nonempty. If $k\ge3$ and
 2881: $r=\lfloor k/2\rfloor$, then
 2882: \begin{equation}\label{eq:private-cover-finite-bounds}
 2883:  (2^r-1)^{k-r}
 2884:  \le \#\mathcal M_n
 2885:  \le \sum_{s=1}^{k}\binom{k}{s}(2R(n))^s2^{s(k-s)}.
 2886: \end{equation}
 2887: In particular, uniformly along every sequence for which $\omega(n)\to\infty$,
 2888: \begin{equation}\label{eq:private-cover-entropy-order}
 2889:  \frac{\log2}{4}\,\omega(n)^2-O(1)
 2890:  \le L(n)
 2891:  \le \frac{\log2}{4}\,\omega(n)^2
 2892:       +\omega(n)\log R(n)+O(\omega(n)).
 2893: \end{equation}
 2894: The coefficient $\log2/4$ is the exact entropy coefficient for the support
 2895: combinatorics. This is not an asymptotic formula for $L(n)$ unless
 2896: $R(n)=\exp(o(\omega(n)))$; no such unconditional estimate for $R(n)$ is
 2897: proved here.
 2898: 
 2899: For comparison, the crude support-count estimate gives
 2900: \begin{equation}
 2901: \label{eq:finite-minimal-generator-bound}
 2902:  \#\mathcal M_n
 2903:  \le \sum_{r=0}^{k}\binom{\Omega(F_n)}{r}
 2904:  \le n^k.
 2905: \end{equation}
 2906: Combining only the last inequality with the classical maximal order of
 2907: $\omega(n)$ gives the crude corollary
 2908: \begin{equation}
 2909: \label{eq:crude-maximal-order}
 2910:  \limsup_{n\to\infty}
 2911:  \frac{L(n)\log\log n}{(\log n)^2}\le1.
 2912: \end{equation}
 2913: This envelope has scale $(\log n)^2/\log\log n$, a factor $\log\log n$
 2914: above the witness-cover entropy scale; it is not a consequence of
 2915: \eqref{eq:private-cover-entropy-order}.
 2916: At the coarser divisor-lattice scale, the antichain property and Wigert's
 2917: theorem give the Sperner-type envelope
 2918: \begin{equation}
 2919: \label{eq:sperner-wigert-envelope}
 2920:  L(n)\le\log\tau(F_n)
 2921:  \le (\log2\,\log\varphi+o(1))\frac{n}{\log n},
 2922:  \qquad \varphi=\frac{1+\sqrt5}{2};
 2923: \end{equation}
 2924: in fact \eqref{eq:crude-maximal-order} is stronger and implies
 2925: $L(n)=o(n/\log n)$.
 2926: 
 2927: Moreover, as $x\to\infty$,
 2928: \begin{equation}
 2929: \label{eq:mean-logarithmic-growth}
 2930:  \left(\frac{\log2}{4}+o(1)\right)x(\log\log x)^2
 2931:  \le \sum_{3\le n\le x}L(n)
 2932:  \le (1+o(1))x\log x\,\log\log x.
 2933: \end{equation}
 2934: 
 2935: \end{theorem}
 2936: 
 2937: \begin{proof}
 2938: First, $F_n\in B_n$ for every $n\ge3$.  Indeed,
 2939: $F_n\mid F_d$ implies $F_n\le F_d$, and strict increase of the Fibonacci
 2940: sequence from index $2$ onward forces $d\ge n$; hence $\alpha(F_n)=n$.
 2941: Because $B_n$ is a nonempty finite poset, it has a minimal element, so
 2942: $\mathcal M_n\ne\varnothing$ and $L(n)$ is defined.
 2943: 
 2944: We first prove the explicit finite private-cover bound in
 2945: \eqref{eq:private-cover-finite-bounds}. Fix $k\ge3$, and give
 2946: $\mathcal P(n)$ an arbitrary order. Put
 2947: \[
 2948:  X=\mathcal P(n)\cap\{2,3\},\qquad r=\lfloor k/2\rfloor.
 2949: \]
 2950: Since $k-\#X\ge k-2\ge r$, choose an $r$-element set
 2951: $P\subseteq\mathcal P(n)\setminus X$, and put
 2952: $Q=\mathcal P(n)\setminus P$. For every map
 2953: \[
 2954:  H:Q\longrightarrow 2^P\setminus\{\varnothing\}
 2955: \]
 2956: and every $i\in P$, define
 2957: \[
 2958:  T_i=\{i\}\cup\{q\in Q:i\in H(q)\}.
 2959: \]
 2960: The index $n_{T_i}=\prod_{\ell\in T_i}\ell^{\nu_\ell(n)}$ has a prime
 2961: divisor outside $\{2,3\}$, because $i\in P$. Hence $n_{T_i}\ge5$ and
 2962: $n_{T_i}\notin\{6,12\}$. Theorem~\ref{thm:primitive-divisors} supplies a
 2963: prime $\pi_{T_i}$ with $\alpha(\pi_{T_i})=n_{T_i}$; fix one such prime for
 2964: each support that occurs.
 2965: 
 2966: The supports $T_i$ cover $\mathcal P(n)$ because $H(q)$ is nonempty for every
 2967: $q\in Q$. Moreover, $i$ belongs to $T_i$ and to no $T_j$ with $j\ne i$.
 2968: Each $\pi_{T_i}$ is a prime atom, so
 2969: $E_n(\pi_{T_i})=T_n(\pi_{T_i})=T_i$. Thus
 2970: $\{\pi_{T_i}:i\in P\}$ is an $n$-witness cover. Distinct supports have
 2971: distinct ranks and therefore distinct underlying primes. If two maps $H$
 2972: differ, then their support families differ, since the edge containing the
 2973: private coordinate $i$ identifies $T_i$; unique factorization then gives
 2974: different minimal generators. There are
 2975: $(2^r-1)^{\#Q}=(2^r-1)^{k-r}$ maps $H$, proving the lower bound in
 2976: \eqref{eq:private-cover-finite-bounds}.
 2977: 
 2978: For the upper bound, let $\Theta$ be an $n$-witness cover with $s$ atoms. For
 2979: each $\theta\in\Theta$, choose the least coordinate
 2980: \[
 2981:  x_\theta\in E_n(\theta)\setminus
 2982:  \bigcup_{\eta\in\Theta\setminus\{\theta\}}T_n(\eta).
 2983: \]
 2984: These private coordinates are distinct; write
 2985: $P=\{x_\theta:\theta\in\Theta\}$ and
 2986: $Q=\mathcal P(n)\setminus P$. If $x_\theta=x$, then
 2987: \[
 2988:  T_n(\theta)=\{x\}\cup U_\theta,\qquad U_\theta\subseteq Q,
 2989: \]
 2990: because every other member of $P$ is private to another atom. By
 2991: Lemma~\ref{thm:primitive-ladder-dichotomy}, either
 2992: $E_n(\theta)=T_n(\theta)$ or $E_n(\theta)=\{x\}$. Hence there are at most two
 2993: support-pair alternatives, and the definition
 2994: \eqref{eq:rank-window-multiplicity} allows at most $R(n)$ atoms for each.
 2995: Thus, once $P$ is fixed, the atom indexed by each
 2996: $x\in P$ has at most $2R(n)2^{k-s}$ choices. Ignoring coverage and
 2997: coprimeness only enlarges the count, so summing over the
 2998: $\binom{k}{s}$ possible sets $P$ and over $1\le s\le k$ proves the upper
 2999: bound in \eqref{eq:private-cover-finite-bounds}.
 3000: 
 3001: Now $r(k-r)=\lfloor k^2/4\rfloor$ and
 3002: $(k-r)\log(1-2^{-r})=O(1)$. Hence the logarithm of the lower bound is
 3003: $(\log2)k^2/4+O(1)$. For the upper bound, use
 3004: $s(k-s)\le k^2/4$, $s\le k$, and
 3005: $\sum_s\binom{k}{s}\le2^k$ to obtain
 3006: \[
 3007:  \log\sum_{s=1}^{k}\binom{k}{s}(2R(n))^s2^{s(k-s)}
 3008:  \le\frac{\log2}{4}k^2+k\log R(n)+O(k).
 3009: \]
 3010: This proves \eqref{eq:private-cover-entropy-order}.
 3011: 
 3012: Let
 3013: \[
 3014:  \mathcal Q_n=\{p^e:p\mid F_n,\ 1\le e\le\nu_p(F_n)\}.
 3015: \]
 3016: This set has cardinality $\Omega(F_n)$.  By unique factorization, the map
 3017: \[
 3018:  m\longmapsto\{p^{\nu_p(m)}:p\mid m\}
 3019: \]
 3020: is injective from $\mathcal M_n$ into the subsets of $\mathcal Q_n$.
 3021: Corollary~\ref{cor:support-bound} says that every image has at most
 3022: $k=\omega(n)$ members.  This proves the first inequality in
 3023: \eqref{eq:finite-minimal-generator-bound}.
 3024: 
 3025: Since every prime factor of $F_n$ is at least $2$,
 3026: $2^{\Omega(F_n)}\le F_n$.  Also $F_n<2^{n-1}$ for $n\ge3$, by the elementary
 3027: induction $F_{j+1}=F_j+F_{j-1}<2^j$.  Hence
 3028: $\Omega(F_n)\le n-2$.  A subset of an $N$-element set having at most $k$
 3029: members can be encoded injectively by its increasing list padded to length
 3030: $k$ with a new symbol; therefore
 3031: \[
 3032:  \sum_{r=0}^k\binom Nr\le(N+1)^k.
 3033: \]
 3034: Taking $N=\Omega(F_n)$ gives the second inequality in
 3035: \eqref{eq:finite-minimal-generator-bound}.
 3036: 
 3037: The classical maximal-order formula for $\omega(n)$
 3038: \cite[Theorem~22.11]{HardyWright2008Introduction}
 3039: \[
 3040:  \limsup_{n\to\infty}
 3041:  \frac{\omega(n)\log\log n}{\log n}=1
 3042: \]
 3043: now combines with $L(n)\le\omega(n)\log n$ to prove
 3044: \eqref{eq:crude-maximal-order}. This argument uses only the crude estimate
 3045: $\#\mathcal M_n\le n^{\omega(n)}$, not the witness-cover entropy bound. On
 3046: the other hand,
 3047: $\mathcal M_n$ is an antichain in the divisor lattice of $F_n$, so
 3048: \[
 3049:  \#\mathcal M_n\le\widthop(\operatorname{Div}(F_n))\le\tau(F_n).
 3050: \]
 3051: Wigert's maximal-order theorem \cite{Wigert1907Divisors} states that
 3052: \[
 3053:  \log\tau(N)\le(\log2+o(1))\frac{\log N}{\log\log N}.
 3054: \]
 3055: Binet's formula gives $\log F_n=n\log\varphi+O(1)$ and
 3056: $\log\log F_n=\log n+O(1)$, yielding
 3057: \eqref{eq:sperner-wigert-envelope}.  Finally,
 3058: $(\log n)^2/\log\log n=o(n/\log n)$, so
 3059: \eqref{eq:crude-maximal-order} gives the stated strict improvement.
 3060: 
 3061: For the mean upper bound, use the classical mean-value estimate
 3062: \cite{HardyRamanujan1917NormalPrimeFactors}
 3063: \begin{equation}
 3064: \label{eq:mean-omega-input}
 3065:  \sum_{n\le x}\omega(n)=x\log\log x+O(x),
 3066: \end{equation}
 3067: together with $L(n)\le\omega(n)\log n$.  Uniformly for $n\le x$,
 3068: $\log n\le\log x$, and hence
 3069: \[
 3070:  \sum_{3\le n\le x}L(n)
 3071:  \le\log x\sum_{n\le x}\omega(n)
 3072:  =(1+o(1))x\log x\,\log\log x.
 3073: \]
 3074: 
 3075: Tur\'an's second-moment form of the Hardy--Ramanujan theorem
 3076: \cite{Turan1934HardyRamanujan} gives
 3077: \begin{equation}\label{eq:omega-second-moment}
 3078:  \sum_{n\le x}\omega(n)^2=(1+o(1))x(\log\log x)^2.
 3079: \end{equation}
 3080: After enlarging the absolute constant in the lower half of
 3081: \eqref{eq:private-cover-entropy-order}, it holds as
 3082: $L(n)\ge(\log2)\omega(n)^2/4-O(1)$ also for $\omega(n)<3$.
 3083: Summation and \eqref{eq:omega-second-moment} prove the lower half of
 3084: \eqref{eq:mean-logarithmic-growth} and complete the proof.
 3085: \end{proof}
 3086: 
 3087: \begin{lemma}[Known baseline for the arithmetic interface]
 3088: \label{lem:R-baseline}
 3089: For every $n\ge3$,
 3090: \[
 3091:  R(n)\le 1+\omega(F_n)=O(n),
 3092:  \qquad
 3093:  \log R(n)\le\log n+O(1).
 3094: \]
 3095: Moreover, Stroi\'nski's cumulative estimate gives
 3096: \[
 3097:  \limsup_{x\to\infty}\frac{\log x}{x^2}
 3098:  \#\{p:\alpha(p)\le x\}
 3099:  \le \frac{3\log\varphi}{2\pi^2}.
 3100: \]
 3101: Neither estimate implies the conjectures below.
 3102: \end{lemma}
 3103: 
 3104: \begin{proof}
 3105: Every prime atom in a family $\mathcal A_{I,J}(n)$ divides $F_n$, and a fixed
 3106: ladder family contains at most one atom. Thus a family contains at most all
 3107: distinct prime divisors of $F_n$ together with one ladder atom, proving
 3108: $R(n)\le1+\omega(F_n)$. Since
 3109: $2^{\omega(F_n)}\le\operatorname{rad}(F_n)\le F_n$ and
 3110: $\log F_n=n\log\varphi+O(1)$, this gives $R(n)=O(n)$ and the logarithmic
 3111: bound. The cumulative estimate is Stroi\'nski's Theorem~7
 3112: \cite{Stroinski2016AlphaContraction}.
 3113: 
 3114: Quantitatively, the normal-order conjecture below asks for
 3115: $\log R(n)=o(\log\log n)$ for almost all $n$, whereas the elementary estimate
 3116: gives only $O(\log n)$. The cumulative estimate controls all primes of rank at
 3117: most $x$ together and likewise gives at best a logarithmic bound of order
 3118: $\log n$ for a single window. For the mean conjecture, the elementary bound
 3119: and the mean order of $\omega$ give only
 3120: $O(x\log x\log\log x)$, not $o(x(\log\log x)^2)$.
 3121: \end{proof}
 3122: 
 3123: \begin{remark}[Cumulative rank estimates do not imply \textup{(H2)}]
 3124: \label{rem:cumulative-rank-estimates-insufficient}
 3125: This nonimplication can be made quantitative without asserting that the model
 3126: below is realized by Fibonacci ranks. Fix $0<c<\log\varphi/2$ and put
 3127: \[
 3128:  a_0(d):=\max\left\{1,
 3129:  \left\lfloor\frac{c\phi_{\rm E}(d)}{\log(2d)}\right\rfloor\right\}.
 3130: \]
 3131: Elementary integral comparison gives
 3132: \[
 3133:  \sum_{d\le y}a_0(d)\ll\frac{y^2}{\log y},\qquad
 3134:  a_0(d)\ll1+\frac{\phi_{\rm E}(d)}{\log(2d)},\qquad
 3135:  \sum_{d\le y}a_0(d)\log d\ll y^2.
 3136: \]
 3137: Thus this formal multiplicity sequence has the cumulative, pointwise, and
 3138: logarithmic-mass scales supplied by the presently known estimates, including
 3139: Theorem~\ref{thm:fibotomic-rank-entropy}. On the other hand, the standard
 3140: lower bound for Euler's function gives
 3141: \[
 3142:  \log a_0(n)=\log n-\log\log n+O(\log\log\log(3n))
 3143: \]
 3144: for large $n$.
 3145: Partial summation in \eqref{eq:mean-omega-input} therefore gives
 3146: \[
 3147:  \sum_{n\le x}\omega(n)\log a_0(n)
 3148:  \sim x\log x\log\log x,
 3149: \]
 3150: which is larger than the scale in \textup{(H2)}. Hence cumulative exact-rank
 3151: counts alone cannot establish the required visible-divisor maximum estimate.
 3152: \end{remark}
 3153: 
 3154: \begin{corollary}[Unconditional lower growth]
 3155: \label{cor:unconditional-lower-growth}
 3156: For almost all $n$,
 3157: \[
 3158:  \log\#\mathcal M_n\ge
 3159:  \left(\frac{\log2}{4}+o(1)\right)(\log\log n)^2.
 3160: \]
 3161: If $N_y:=\prod_{p\le y}p$, then
 3162: \[
 3163:  \log\#\mathcal M_{N_y}\ge
 3164:  \left(\frac{\log2}{4}+o(1)\right)
 3165:  \frac{(\log N_y)^2}{(\log\log N_y)^2}.
 3166: \]
 3167: \end{corollary}
 3168: 
 3169: \begin{proof}
 3170: The Hardy--Ramanujan normal-order theorem states that
 3171: $\omega(n)=(1+o(1))\log\log n$ for almost all $n$
 3172: \cite{HardyRamanujan1917NormalPrimeFactors,%
 3173: Tenenbaum2015AnalyticProbabilistic}. Substitution in the lower half of
 3174: \eqref{eq:private-cover-entropy-order} proves the first assertion. The prime
 3175: number theorem gives
 3176: $\omega(N_y)=\pi(y)\sim y/\log y\sim\log N_y/\log\log N_y$; substituting this
 3177: in the same lower bound proves the second assertion.
 3178: \end{proof}
 3179: 
 3180: \begin{conjecture}[Normal rank-window sparsity]\label{hyp:normal-window}
 3181: For every $\varepsilon>0$,
 3182: \[
 3183:  \#\{n\le x:\log R(n)>\varepsilon\omega(n)\}=o(x).
 3184: \tag{H1}
 3185: \]
 3186: \end{conjecture}
 3187: 
 3188: \begin{conjecture}[Mean sparse-window hypothesis]\label{hyp:mean-window}
 3189: One has
 3190: \[
 3191:  \sum_{3\le n\le x}\omega(n)\log R(n)
 3192:  =o\bigl(x(\log\log x)^2\bigr).
 3193: \tag{H2}
 3194: \]
 3195: \end{conjecture}
 3196: 
 3197: \begin{proposition}[Conditional obstruction from Fibonacci prime divisors]
 3198: \label{prop:blms-obstructs-mean-window}
 3199: Assume Conjecture~5.1 of Bugeaud--Luca--Mignotte--Siksek
 3200: \cite{BugeaudLucaMignotteSiksek2005FewPrimeDivisors}, namely that
 3201: $\omega(F_n)\gg\log n$ for composite $n$. Then
 3202: \begin{equation}\label{eq:blms-mean-window-obstruction}
 3203:  \liminf_{x\to\infty}
 3204:  \frac{\sum_{3\le n\le x}\omega(n)\log R(n)}
 3205:  {x(\log\log x)^2}
 3206:  \ge\frac6{\pi^2}(1-\log2)>0.
 3207: \end{equation}
 3208: In particular, under that published conjecture, the mean sparse-window
 3209: hypothesis \textup{(H2)} is false.
 3210: \end{proposition}
 3211: 
 3212: \begin{proof}
 3213: Let $n$ be squarefree and put $k=\omega(n)$. The exact-rank partition of the
 3214: prime divisors of $F_n$ gives
 3215: \[
 3216:  \omega(F_n)=\sum_{d\mid n}a(d).
 3217: \]
 3218: For squarefree $n$, every nonempty support $J\subseteq\mathcal P(n)$ has the
 3219: single rank $d=\prod_{\ell\in J}\ell$ in its window. Since $a(1)=0$, the
 3220: pigeonhole principle and \eqref{eq:prime-window-versus-R} give
 3221: \begin{equation}\label{eq:squarefree-blms-pigeonhole}
 3222:  R(n)\ge\max_{d\mid n}a(d)
 3223:  \ge\frac{\omega(F_n)}{2^k-1}.
 3224: \end{equation}
 3225: Under the stated conjecture, for every sufficiently large squarefree composite
 3226: $n$,
 3227: \[
 3228:  \log R(n)\ge\log\log n-k\log2+O(1).
 3229: \]
 3230: The Hardy--Ramanujan theorem has an exceptional set of $o(x)$ integers, so on
 3231: all but $o(x)$ squarefree integers one has
 3232: $k=(1+o(1))\log\log n$. Consequently
 3233: \[
 3234:  \omega(n)\log R(n)
 3235:  \ge(1-\log2+o(1))(\log\log n)^2
 3236: \]
 3237: on this set. Squarefree integers have density $6/\pi^2$, and squarefree prime
 3238: indices have density zero. Summation over squarefree composite indices proves
 3239: \eqref{eq:blms-mean-window-obstruction}.
 3240: \end{proof}
 3241: 
 3242: \begin{corollary}[Conditional normal and mean orders]
 3243: \label{cor:conditional-orders}
 3244: Assuming Conjecture~\ref{hyp:normal-window},
 3245: \begin{equation}\label{eq:conditional-normal-order}
 3246:  L(n)\sim\frac{\log2}{4}(\log\log n)^2
 3247:  \quad\text{for almost all }n.
 3248: \end{equation}
 3249: Assuming Conjecture~\ref{hyp:mean-window},
 3250: \begin{equation}\label{eq:conditional-mean-order}
 3251:  \sum_{3\le n\le x}L(n)
 3252:  \sim\frac{\log2}{4}x(\log\log x)^2.
 3253: \end{equation}
 3254: Neither conclusion is unconditional.
 3255: \end{corollary}
 3256: 
 3257: \begin{proof}
 3258: For the first assertion, intersect the density-one set in
 3259: Conjecture~\ref{hyp:normal-window} with the Hardy--Ramanujan normal-order set.
 3260: Then the error $\omega(n)\log R(n)+O(\omega(n))$ in
 3261: \eqref{eq:private-cover-entropy-order} is $o(\omega(n)^2)$. For the second,
 3262: sum the upper bound in \eqref{eq:private-cover-entropy-order}, use
 3263: Conjecture~\ref{hyp:mean-window}, \eqref{eq:omega-second-moment}, and
 3264: $\sum_{n\le x}\omega(n)=O(x\log\log x)$, and compare with the unconditional
 3265: lower mean bound \eqref{eq:mean-logarithmic-growth}.
 3266: \end{proof}
 3267: 
 3268: \begin{remark}[Exact residual arithmetic interface]
 3269: \label{rem:exact-average-open-interface}
 3270: Corollary~\ref{cor:theta-refined-connected-entropy} and
 3271: Theorem~\ref{thm:minimal-generator-growth} determine the currently available
 3272: support contribution: $\log2/4$ is its exact quadratic coefficient, the
 3273: rank-pure sector supplies its theta-refined lower terms, and the unresolved
 3274: term in the upper bound is $\omega(n)\log R(n)$. The rank partition used in
 3275: Lemma~\ref{lem:three-coordinate-arithmetic-tests} applies verbatim to every
 3276: support size: prime-atom families are finite disjoint unions of the rank
 3277: classes $\Pi_\alpha(d)$, while ladder families are singletons. Thus
 3278: Conjectures~\ref{hyp:normal-window} and
 3279: \ref{hyp:mean-window} ask precisely for upper distributional control of
 3280: exact-rank prime multiplicities in those rank windows.
 3281: 
 3282: The pointwise estimate \eqref{eq:pointwise-exact-rank-bound} gives new control
 3283: of each individual class, but
 3284: Remark~\ref{rem:cumulative-rank-estimates-insufficient} shows that even its
 3285: Euler-factor scale does not by itself imply either rank-window conjecture. In
 3286: particular, no optimality claim about all possible uses of fibotomic
 3287: information is needed here.
 3288: 
 3289: The primitive-divisor theorems of Carmichael and Bilu--Hanrot--Voutier, and
 3290: the refinements and size results of Kiss, Granville, and Hong
 3291: \cite{Kiss1988PrimitiveDivisors,Granville2012PrimitivePrimeFactors,%
 3292: Hong2025BigPrimitiveDivisors}, give existence or information about the size of
 3293: primitive divisors, not the required upper multiplicity estimates. The
 3294: Hardy--Ramanujan and
 3295: Tur\'an theorems control the additive function $\omega(n)$ but not the prime
 3296: factorization of $F_d$ across exact apparition ranks. Likewise, the
 3297: Erd\H{o}s--Wintner theorem \cite{ErdosWintner1939Additive} concerns limiting
 3298: distributions of additive arithmetic functions, whereas $L(n)$ and $R(n)$
 3299: are not additive. No implication from RH or ERH to either rank-window
 3300: conjecture is presently known. Accordingly, the normal and mean equivalents
 3301: \eqref{eq:conditional-normal-order} and
 3302: \eqref{eq:conditional-mean-order} are conditional; the unconditional
 3303: statements are \eqref{eq:private-cover-entropy-order} and
 3304: \eqref{eq:mean-logarithmic-growth}, together with the stronger connected lower
 3305: bound \eqref{eq:theta-connected-log-lower-bound}. None of these estimates
 3306: implies that the disconnected part of $\mathcal M_n$ is negligible; the
 3307: strictly stronger blockwise hypothesis needed for that conclusion is isolated
 3308: in Theorem~\ref{thm:blockwise-connected-dominance} below.
 3309: \end{remark}
 3310: 
 3311: \begin{lemma}[Refined private-coordinate upper bound]
 3312: \label{lem:refined-private-coordinate-upper-bound}
 3313: For $m\ge2$, put $s=\omega(m)$ and $r=R(m)$. Then
 3314: \begin{equation}\label{eq:refined-private-coordinate-upper-bound}
 3315:  \#\mathcal M_m\le U_s(r),\qquad
 3316:  U_s(r):=\sum_{t=1}^s\binom st
 3317:              \bigl(r2^{s-t}+1\bigr)^t.
 3318: \end{equation}
 3319: There is an absolute constant $K$ such that, for all $s,r\ge1$,
 3320: \begin{equation}\label{eq:refined-upper-versus-covers}
 3321:  U_s(r)\le Kr^sC_s.
 3322: \end{equation}
 3323: \end{lemma}
 3324: 
 3325: \begin{proof}
 3326: The case $m=2$ is immediate from $\mathcal M_2=\varnothing$ and $R(2)=1$.
 3327: Suppose $m\ge3$, and let a witness cover have $t$ atoms. Choose the canonical
 3328: private coordinate of each atom as in the proof of
 3329: Theorem~\ref{thm:minimal-generator-growth}; let $P$ be the resulting
 3330: $t$-set and $Q$ its complement. For a fixed $x\in P$, a prime atom has full
 3331: support $\{x\}\cup U$ for some $U\subseteq Q$, giving at most

   31: \title{Upper fibers, witness covers, and support entropy for
   32: Fibonacci ranks of apparition}
   33: \author{
   34: Haobo Ma\\
   35: AELF PTE LTD.\\
   36: \#14-02, Marina Bay Financial Centre Tower 1, 8 Marina Blvd\\
   37: Singapore 018981, Singapore\\
   38: \texttt{auric@aelf.io}
