 2261: \section{Support entropy and the unresolved arithmetic multiplicity}
 2262: \label{sec:growth}
 2263: %% ====================================================================
 2264: 
 2265: Write $\Omega(N)=\sum_p\nu_p(N)$ for the number of prime factors of $N$
 2266: counted with multiplicity. Set $R(2)=1$, consistently with
 2267: $\mathcal M_2=\varnothing$. For $n\ge3$ define the maximal atomic rank-window
 2268: multiplicity
 2269: \begin{equation}\label{eq:rank-window-multiplicity}
 2270:  R(n):=\max\!\left\{1,
 2271:  \max_{\varnothing\ne I\subseteq J\subseteq\mathcal P(n)}
 2272:        \#\mathcal A_{I,J}(n)\right\}.
 2273: \end{equation}
 2274: By the prime--ladder alternative, the nonsingleton families counted here are the
 2275: exact-rank windows $P_J(n)$, whereas every ladder family contributes at most
 2276: one atom. Thus $R(n)$ records exactly the arithmetic multiplicity not fixed by
 2277: the support combinatorics.
 2278: 
 2279: We first remove the contribution caused solely by collecting several exact
 2280: rank classes in one support window. For $d\mid n$, write
 2281: \[
 2282:  T_n(d):=\{\ell\in\mathcal P(n):
 2283:  \nu_\ell(d)=\nu_\ell(n)\}.
 2284: \]
 2285: 
 2286: \begin{theorem}[Mean rank-window deaggregation]
 2287: \label{thm:mean-rank-window-deaggregation}
 2288: Put
 2289: \[
 2290:  a(d):=\#\Pi_\alpha(d),\qquad
 2291:  \mathcal D^+(n):=\{d\mid n:T_n(d)\ne\varnothing\},
 2292: \]
 2293: and
 2294: \[
 2295:  A^*(n):=\max\left\{1,\max_{d\in\mathcal D^+(n)}a(d)\right\}.
 2296: \]
 2297: Then, as $x\to\infty$,
 2298: \begin{equation}\label{eq:mean-window-deaggregation}
 2299:  \sum_{3\le n\le x}\omega(n)\log R(n)
 2300:  =\sum_{3\le n\le x}\omega(n)\log A^*(n)
 2301:  +O(x\log\log x).
 2302: \end{equation}
 2303: Consequently the mean sparse-window hypothesis \textup{(H2)} is equivalent to
 2304: \begin{equation}\label{eq:visible-single-rank-interface}
 2305:  \sum_{3\le n\le x}\omega(n)
 2306:  \log\max\left\{1,
 2307:  \max_{\substack{d\mid n\\T_n(d)\ne\varnothing}}
 2308:  \#\Pi_\alpha(d)\right\}
 2309:  =o\bigl(x(\log\log x)^2\bigr).
 2310: \end{equation}
 2311: \end{theorem}
 2312: 
 2313: \begin{proof}
 2314: For nonempty $J\subseteq\mathcal P(n)$, let
 2315: \[
 2316:  W_J(n):=\{d\mid n:T_n(d)=J\}.
 2317: \]
 2318: If $n=\prod_\ell\ell^{e_\ell}$, then
 2319: \begin{equation}\label{eq:support-window-size}
 2320:  \#W_J(n)=\prod_{\ell\notin J}e_\ell.
 2321: \end{equation}
 2322: The prime atoms in the family with essential and full supports $(J,J)$ form
 2323: the disjoint union
 2324: \[
 2325:  \coprod_{d\in W_J(n)}\Pi_\alpha(d),
 2326: \]
 2327: because a prime has a unique rank of apparition. By
 2328: Lemma~\ref{thm:primitive-ladder-dichotomy}, every remaining effective atomic
 2329: family is a ladder family with singleton essential support. Once that
 2330: essential coordinate $\ell$ is fixed, the underlying prime is $\ell$, and
 2331: the full-exponent equation in Proposition~\ref{prop:prime-power-ladders}
 2332: determines at most one ladder exponent. Therefore, with
 2333: \[
 2334:  P(n):=\max_{\varnothing\ne J\subseteq\mathcal P(n)}
 2335:        \sum_{d\in W_J(n)}a(d),
 2336: \]
 2337: one has
 2338: \begin{equation}\label{eq:prime-window-versus-R}
 2339:  P(n)\le R(n)\le P(n)+1.
 2340: \end{equation}
 2341: Every $d\in\mathcal D^+(n)$ belongs to one of the windows above. Hence
 2342: \[
 2343:  A^*(n)\le R(n)
 2344:  \le1+A^*(n)\max_J\#W_J(n)
 2345:  \le1+A^*(n)\prod_{\ell^{e_\ell}\parallel n}e_\ell.
 2346: \]
 2347: Writing
 2348: \[
 2349:  h(n):=\sum_{\ell^{e_\ell}\parallel n}\log e_\ell,
 2350: \]
 2351: and using $A^*(n)\ge1$ gives
 2352: \begin{equation}\label{eq:pointwise-window-deaggregation}
 2353:  0\le\log R(n)-\log A^*(n)\le\log2+h(n).
 2354: \end{equation}
 2355: For each exponent $e\ge2$, $\log e\le\sum_{2\le j\le e}\log j$, so
 2356: \[
 2357:  h(n)\le\sum_{\substack{p^j\mid n\\j\ge2}}\log j.
 2358: \]
 2359: The classical mean estimate \eqref{eq:mean-omega-input}, used uniformly with
 2360: $\log\log(3y)$ in place of $\log\log y$ for small $y$, yields
 2361: \begin{align*}
 2362:  \sum_{n\le x}\omega(n)h(n)
 2363:  &\le\sum_{\substack{p^j\le x\\j\ge2}}\log j
 2364:        \sum_{m\le x/p^j}\omega(p^jm)\\
 2365:  &\le\sum_{\substack{p^j\le x\\j\ge2}}\log j
 2366:        \sum_{m\le x/p^j}(1+\omega(m))\\
 2367:  &\ll x\log\log x\sum_p\sum_{j\ge2}\frac{\log j}{p^j}
 2368:  \ll x\log\log x.
 2369: \end{align*}
 2370: The double series converges absolutely. Summing
 2371: \eqref{eq:pointwise-window-deaggregation} and using
 2372: $\sum_{n\le x}\omega(n)\ll x\log\log x$ proves
 2373: \eqref{eq:mean-window-deaggregation}; the stated equivalence follows because
 2374: $x\log\log x=o(x(\log\log x)^2)$.
 2375: \end{proof}
 2376: 
 2377: \begin{corollary}[Single-rank sufficient and necessary interfaces]
 2378: \label{cor:single-rank-mean-interfaces}
 2379: A sufficient condition for \textup{(H2)} is
 2380: \begin{equation}\label{eq:single-rank-sufficient-condition}
 2381:  \sum_{d\le x}
 2382:  \frac{(\omega(d)+\log\log x)\log\max\{1,a(d)\}}{d}
 2383:  =o\bigl((\log\log x)^2\bigr).
 2384: \end{equation}
 2385: Conversely, \textup{(H2)} implies the diagonal necessary condition
 2386: \begin{equation}\label{eq:single-rank-necessary-condition}
 2387:  \sum_{n\le x}\omega(n)\log\max\{1,a(n)\}
 2388:  =o\bigl(x(\log\log x)^2\bigr).
 2389: \end{equation}
 2390: \end{corollary}
 2391: 
 2392: \begin{proof}
 2393: Since
 2394: \[
 2395:  \log A^*(n)\le\sum_{d\mid n}\log\max\{1,a(d)\},
 2396: \]
 2397: the substitution $n=dm$ and $\omega(dm)\le\omega(d)+\omega(m)$ gives
 2398: \[
 2399:  \sum_{n\le x}\omega(n)\log A^*(n)
 2400:  \ll x\sum_{d\le x}
 2401:  \frac{(\omega(d)+\log\log x)\log\max\{1,a(d)\}}{d}.
 2402: \]
 2403: Thus \eqref{eq:single-rank-sufficient-condition} and
 2404: Theorem~\ref{thm:mean-rank-window-deaggregation} imply \textup{(H2)}. For the
 2405: necessary condition, $n\in\mathcal D^+(n)$, and hence
 2406: $R(n)\ge A^*(n)\ge\max\{1,a(n)\}$; summation proves the claim.
 2407: \end{proof}
 2408: 
 2409: \begin{theorem}[Fibotomic rank entropy]
 2410: \label{thm:fibotomic-rank-entropy}
 2411: Let $\varphi=(1+\sqrt5)/2$, let $\phi_{\rm E}$ denote Euler's totient
 2412: function, and, for $d\geq3$, put
 2413: \[
 2414:  \mathfrak F_d:=\prod_{r\mid d}F_r^{\mu(d/r)}.
 2415: \]
 2416: For $k\geq0$, set $m=\lfloor k/2\rfloor$ and
 2417: \[
 2418:  \mathcal H_d(k):=
 2419:  k\log\frac{2d}{3}+2\log(m!)+(k-2m)\log(m+1).
 2420: \]
 2421: There is an absolute constant $C_F$ such that, for every $d\geq3$,
 2422: \begin{equation}\label{eq:fibotomic-rank-entropy}
 2423:  \mathcal H_d(a(d))\leq \phi_{\rm E}(d)\log\varphi+C_F.
 2424: \end{equation}
 2425: More generally, every finitely supported family of nonnegative real numbers
 2426: $(\lambda_d)_{d\geq3}$ satisfies
 2427: \begin{equation}\label{eq:weighted-fibotomic-rank-entropy}
 2428:  \sum_{d\geq3}\lambda_d\mathcal H_d(a(d))
 2429:  \leq (\log\varphi)\sum_{d\geq3}\lambda_d\phi_{\rm E}(d)
 2430:        +C_F\sum_{d\geq3}\lambda_d.
 2431: \end{equation}
 2432: Uniformly as $d\to\infty$,
 2433: \begin{equation}\label{eq:pointwise-exact-rank-bound}
 2434:  a(d)\leq
 2435:  \left(\frac{\log\varphi}{2}+o(1)\right)
 2436:  \frac{\phi_{\rm E}(d)}{\log d}.
 2437: \end{equation}
 2438: Consequently, uniformly as $n\to\infty$,
 2439: \begin{equation}\label{eq:visible-exact-rank-bound}
 2440:  A^*(n)\leq
 2441:  \left(\frac{\log\varphi}{2}+o(1)\right)
 2442:  \frac{\phi_{\rm E}(n)}{\log n}.
 2443: \end{equation}
 2444: \end{theorem}
 2445: 
 2446: \begin{proof}
 2447: Let $\Psi_d(x)$ be the $d$th fibotomic polynomial. The fibotomic
 2448: factorization of Byer--Dvorachek--Eckard--Harrington--Wise--Wong
 2449: \cite{CameronCountsLundMillerPiechnikWong2022Fibotomic} gives
 2450: \[
 2451:  F_d(x)=\prod_{r\mid d}\Psi_r(x),
 2452:  \qquad
 2453:  \mathfrak F_d=\Psi_d(1)\in\mathbb N.
 2454: \]
 2455: This published factorization is used as an input and is not asserted as a
 2456: result of the present paper.
 2457: Binet's formula, in the form
 2458: \[
 2459:  F_r=\frac{\varphi^r}{\sqrt5}
 2460:  \bigl(1-(-\varphi^{-2})^r\bigr),
 2461: \]
 2462: and the identities
 2463: $\sum_{r\mid d}\mu(d/r)=0$ and
 2464: $\sum_{r\mid d}r\mu(d/r)=\phi_{\rm E}(d)$ show that
 2465: \begin{equation}\label{eq:fibotomic-binet-error}
 2466:  \log\mathfrak F_d=\phi_{\rm E}(d)\log\varphi+E_d,
 2467:  \qquad
 2468:  |E_d|\leq
 2469:  C_F:=\sum_{j\geq1}
 2470:  \left|\log\bigl(1-(-\varphi^{-2})^j\bigr)\right|.
 2471: \end{equation}
 2472: The series defining $C_F$ converges geometrically.
 2473: 
 2474: If $p\in\Pi_\alpha(d)$, then $p\mid F_d$ but $p\nmid F_r$ for every
 2475: proper divisor $r$ of $d$. Since
 2476: $F_d=\prod_{r\mid d}\mathfrak F_r$ and each factor is an integer, $p$ must
 2477: divide $\mathfrak F_d$. Distinct exact-rank primes are coprime, and hence
 2478: \begin{equation}\label{eq:exact-rank-radical-divides-fibotomic}
 2479:  \prod_{p\in\Pi_\alpha(d)}p\mid\mathfrak F_d.
 2480: \end{equation}
 2481: For $p\notin\{2,5\}$, the classical rank congruence gives
 2482: \[
 2483:  d=\alpha(p)\mid p-\left(\frac5p\right),
 2484: \]
 2485: see \cite{Wall1960FibonacciModm,Vinson1963RankApparition}. Thus
 2486: $p\equiv\pm1\pmod d$. The exceptional ranks $d=3,5$ have respectively
 2487: the single exact-rank primes $2,5$ and satisfy the estimate directly. For
 2488: every other $d$, write the exact-rank primes in increasing order as
 2489: $p_1<\cdots<p_k$. There are at most two candidates in each pair of residue
 2490: classes $jd\pm1$, whence
 2491: \[
 2492:  p_i\geq d\left\lceil\frac i2\right\rceil-1
 2493:  \geq\frac{2d}{3}\left\lceil\frac i2\right\rceil.
 2494: \]
 2495: It follows that, with $m=\lfloor k/2\rfloor$,
 2496: \begin{equation}\label{eq:rank-congruence-product-bound}
 2497:  \prod_{i=1}^kp_i\geq
 2498:  \left(\frac{2d}{3}\right)^k(m!)^2(m+1)^{k-2m}.
 2499: \end{equation}
 2500: Combining \eqref{eq:fibotomic-binet-error}--
 2501: \eqref{eq:rank-congruence-product-bound} and taking logarithms proves
 2502: \eqref{eq:fibotomic-rank-entropy}. Multiplication by $\lambda_d\geq0$ and
 2503: finite summation proves \eqref{eq:weighted-fibotomic-rank-entropy}.
 2504: 
 2505: Stirling's lower bound applied in \eqref{eq:fibotomic-rank-entropy} gives,
 2506: with an absolute implied constant,
 2507: \begin{equation}\label{eq:rank-entropy-coarse}
 2508:  a(d)\bigl(\log d+\log\max\{1,a(d)\}-O(1)\bigr)
 2509:  \leq \phi_{\rm E}(d)\log\varphi+O(1).
 2510: \end{equation}
 2511: If $a(d)\leq\phi_{\rm E}(d)/(\log d)^2$, then
 2512: \eqref{eq:pointwise-exact-rank-bound} follows immediately. Otherwise the
 2513: standard uniform lower bound
 2514: $\phi_{\rm E}(d)\gg d/\log\log(3d)$
 2515: \cite{HardyWright2008Introduction} implies
 2516: \[
 2517:  \log a(d)=\log d+O(\log\log d)=\log d+o(\log d).
 2518: \]
 2519: Substitution in \eqref{eq:rank-entropy-coarse} yields
 2520: $(2+o(1))a(d)\log d\leq\phi_{\rm E}(d)\log\varphi$, proving
 2521: \eqref{eq:pointwise-exact-rank-bound} uniformly.
 2522: 
 2523: For \eqref{eq:visible-exact-rank-bound}, the case $A^*(n)=1$ follows from
 2524: $\phi_{\rm E}(n)/\log n\to\infty$. Otherwise choose
 2525: $d_n\in\mathcal D^+(n)$ with $A^*(n)=a(d_n)$. The preceding estimate has
 2526: the coarse consequence $a(d)\ll d/\log(2d)$. If

 succeeded in 370ms:
 2527: $d_n\leq n/(\log n)^3$, the lower bound for $\phi_{\rm E}(n)$ gives
 2528: \[
 2529:  a(d_n)=o\left(\frac{\phi_{\rm E}(n)}{\log n}\right).
 2530: \]
 2531: If $d_n>n/(\log n)^3$, then
 2532: $\log d_n=(1+o(1))\log n$ and, since $d_n\mid n$,
 2533: $\phi_{\rm E}(d_n)\leq\phi_{\rm E}(n)$. Applying
 2534: \eqref{eq:pointwise-exact-rank-bound} at $d_n$ completes the proof.
 2535: \end{proof}
 2536: 
 2537: \begin{corollary}[Bounds for the visible single-rank contribution]
 2538: \label{cor:visible-rank-sum-bounds}
 2539: Put
 2540: \[
 2541:  S(x):=\sum_{3\leq n\leq x}\omega(n)\log A^*(n).
 2542: \]
 2543: Then
 2544: \begin{equation}\label{eq:visible-rank-sum-upper}
 2545:  S(x)\leq
 2546:  \sum_{3\leq n\leq x}\omega(n)\log\phi_{\rm E}(n)
 2547:  -x(\log\log x)^2+O(x\log\log x),
 2548: \end{equation}
 2549: whereas
 2550: \begin{equation}\label{eq:visible-rank-sum-lower}
 2551:  S(x)\geq
 2552:  \left(\frac{2\log2}{25}+o(1)\right)x\log\log x.
 2553: \end{equation}
 2554: Neither estimate settles \eqref{eq:visible-single-rank-interface}.
 2555: \end{corollary}
 2556: 
 2557: \begin{proof}
 2558: Taking logarithms in \eqref{eq:visible-exact-rank-bound}, uniformly for
 2559: large $n$, gives
 2560: \[
 2561:  \log A^*(n)\leq
 2562:  \log\phi_{\rm E}(n)-\log\log n
 2563:  +\log\frac{\log\varphi}{2}+o(1).
 2564: \]
 2565: In addition to \eqref{eq:mean-omega-input}, partial summation gives
 2566: \[
 2567:  \sum_{3\leq n\leq x}\omega(n)\log\log n
 2568:  =x(\log\log x)^2+O(x\log\log x).
 2569: \]
 2570: For example, on $\sqrt x<n\leq x$ one may replace $\log\log n$ by
 2571: $\log\log x$ with $O(1)$ error, while the complementary range contributes
 2572: $o(x)$. Summation proves \eqref{eq:visible-rank-sum-upper}.
 2573: 
 2574: For the lower bound, Jarden proved that $L_{5p}$ has at least two distinct
 2575: primitive prime divisors for every prime $p>5$
 2576: \cite{Jarden1968LucasTwoPrimitive}; this published theorem is used as an
 2577: input and is not asserted as a result of the present paper. If $q$ is one of
 2578: them, then
 2579: $q\mid L_{5p}$ and
 2580: \[
 2581:  F_{10p}=F_{5p}L_{5p},\qquad \gcd(F_{5p},L_{5p})\mid2.
 2582: \]
 2583: Here $q$ is odd, so the gcd identity gives $q\nmid F_{5p}$. Thus
 2584: $\alpha(q)=2r$ for some $r\mid5p$. If $r<5p$, then
 2585: $F_{2r}=F_rL_r$ and $q\nmid F_r$, so $q\mid L_r$, contrary to the
 2586: primitivity of $q$ for $L_{5p}$.
 2587: Consequently $\alpha(q)=10p$, and therefore
 2588: \begin{equation}\label{eq:jarden-exact-rank-consequence}
 2589:  a(10p)\geq2\qquad(p>5\ \hbox{prime}).
 2590: \end{equation}
 2591: 
 2592: Let $n=10m$, where $5\nmid m$ and $m$ has a prime factor $p>5$. Then
 2593: $d=10p$ divides $n$ and
 2594: $\nu_5(d)=\nu_5(n)=1$, so $T_n(d)\ne\varnothing$. By
 2595: \eqref{eq:jarden-exact-rank-consequence}, $A^*(n)\geq2$. The excluded
 2596: integers $m$ having no prime factor greater than $5$ are of the form
 2597: $2^a3^b$ and contribute only $O((\log x)^3)$ after weighting by $\omega$.
 2598: Writing $y=x/10$ and interchanging two finite sums gives
 2599: \[
 2600:  \sum_{\substack{m\leq y\\5\nmid m}}\omega(m)
 2601:  =\sum_{\substack{q\leq y\\q\ \mathrm{prime},\ q\ne5}}
 2602:  \left(\left\lfloor\frac yq\right\rfloor
 2603:  -\left\lfloor\frac y{5q}\right\rfloor\right)
 2604:  =\frac45y\log\log y+O(y).
 2605: \]
 2606: Since $\omega(10m)=\omega(m)+O(1)$, the total $\omega$-weight of the
 2607: constructed integers is
 2608: $(2/25)x\log\log x+O(x)$. Multiplication by $\log2$ proves
 2609: \eqref{eq:visible-rank-sum-lower}.
 2610: \end{proof}
 2611: 
 2612: 
 2613: For a finite set $V$, let $\operatorname{MinCov}(V)$ denote the set of
 2614: minimal, or irredundant, covers of $V$ by nonempty subsets:
 2615: \[
 2616:  \operatorname{MinCov}(V):=
 2617:  \left\{\mathcal C\subseteq 2^V\setminus\{\varnothing\}:
 2618:  \bigcup_{S\in\mathcal C}S=V,\quad
 2619:  S\nsubseteq\bigcup_{T\in\mathcal C\setminus\{S\}}T
 2620:  \ \text{for every }S\in\mathcal C\right\}.
 2621: \]
 2622: 
 2623: \begin{theorem}[Rank-pure universality]\label{thm:rank-pure-universality}
 2624: Let $n\ge3$, put $V=\mathcal P(n)$, and, for every nonempty $S\subseteq V$,
 2625: define
 2626: \[
 2627:  n_S:=\prod_{\ell\in S}\ell^{\nu_\ell(n)},\qquad
 2628:  a_n(S):=\#\Pi_\alpha(n_S),
 2629: \]
 2630: and
 2631: \[
 2632:  \mathcal E_n:=\{S\subseteq V:S\ne\varnothing,\ n_S\in\{2,6,12\}\}.
 2633: \]
 2634: Then $\#\mathcal E_n\le2$, and every member of $\mathcal E_n$ has cardinality
 2635: at most two. Define the rank-pure prime sector
 2636: \[
 2637:  \mathcal R_n^{\rm rp}:=
 2638:  \left\{
 2639:  \prod_{S\in\mathcal C}p_S:
 2640:  \begin{array}{l}
 2641:   \mathcal C\in\operatorname{MinCov}(V),\quad
 2642:   \mathcal C\cap\mathcal E_n=\varnothing,\\
 2643:   p_S\in\Pi_\alpha(n_S)\quad(S\in\mathcal C)
 2644:  \end{array}
 2645:  \right\}.
 2646: \]
 2647: Then $\mathcal R_n^{\rm rp}\subseteq\mathcal M_n$, the displayed representation is
 2648: unique, and
 2649: \begin{equation}\label{eq:rank-pure-weighted-sector}
 2650:  \#\mathcal R_n^{\rm rp}=
 2651:  \sum_{\substack{\mathcal C\in\operatorname{MinCov}(V)\\
 2652:                    \mathcal C\cap\mathcal E_n=\varnothing}}
 2653:  \prod_{S\in\mathcal C}a_n(S),
 2654:  \qquad
 2655:  a_n(S)=\sum_{d\mid n_S}\mu(n_S/d)\,\omega(F_d).
 2656: \end{equation}
 2657: Moreover, the support hypergraph of the product attached to $\mathcal C$ has
 2658: edge set exactly $\mathcal C$. In particular, connected minimal covers map to
 2659: $\mathcal M_n^{\rm conn}$, with their complete hypergraph structure preserved.
 2660: \end{theorem}
 2661: 
 2662: \begin{proof}
 2663: The map $S\mapsto n_S$ is injective. If $n_S=2$, then
 2664: $S=\{2\}$ and $\nu_2(n)=1$. If $n_S=6$, then
 2665: $S=\{2,3\}$ and $\nu_2(n)=\nu_3(n)=1$; if $n_S=12$, then
 2666: $S=\{2,3\}$, $\nu_2(n)=2$, and $\nu_3(n)=1$. Thus the ranks $6$ and $12$
 2667: cannot both occur, while rank $2$ can coexist only with rank $6$. This proves
 2668: the assertions about $\mathcal E_n$.
 2669: 
 2670: Fix $\mathcal C\in\operatorname{MinCov}(V)$ avoiding $\mathcal E_n$. For
 2671: every $S\in\mathcal C$, the integer $n_S$ is at least $3$ and is not $6$ or
 2672: $12$. Theorem~\ref{thm:primitive-divisors} therefore gives a prime
 2673: $p_S\in\Pi_\alpha(n_S)$. Distinct supports have distinct ranks, so the primes
 2674: $p_S$ are distinct. Since $p_S$ is a prime atom and $d^-(p_S)=1$,
 2675: \[
 2676:  E_n(p_S)=T_n(p_S)=S.
 2677: \]
 2678: Consequently the coverage and private-coordinate conditions defining
 2679: $\operatorname{MinCov}(V)$ are exactly the witness-cover conditions for
 2680: $\{p_S:S\in\mathcal C\}$. Theorem~\ref{thm:witness-classification} places
 2681: $\prod_{S\in\mathcal C}p_S$ in $\mathcal M_n$.
 2682: 
 2683: Unique factorization recovers every selected prime from the product; its rank
 2684: $\alpha(p_S)=n_S$ then recovers $S$. Hence both the cover and all prime choices
 2685: are recovered, proving injectivity and the weighted count in
 2686: \eqref{eq:rank-pure-weighted-sector}. The formula for $a_n(S)$ is
 2687: Lemma~\ref{lem:primitive-prime-mobius-count}. Finally,
 2688: \[
 2689:  \operatorname{supp}_n(\alpha(p_S))=
 2690:  \operatorname{supp}_n(n_S)=S,
 2691: \]
 2692: so Definition~\ref{def:support-hypergraph} gives precisely the edge set
 2693: $\mathcal C$ and therefore preserves connected components.
 2694: \end{proof}
 2695: 
 2696: \begin{corollary}[Odd-layer cover universality]
 2697: \label{cor:odd-rank-pure-universality}
 2698: Let $n\ge3$ be odd and put $k=\omega(n)$. Then $\mathcal E_n=\varnothing$.
 2699: After fixing one prime $\pi_S\in\Pi_\alpha(n_S)$ for every nonempty
 2700: $S\subseteq\mathcal P(n)$, the map
 2701: \[
 2702:  \mathcal C\longmapsto\prod_{S\in\mathcal C}\pi_S
 2703: \]
 2704: embeds $\operatorname{MinCov}(\mathcal P(n))$ into $\mathcal M_n$ and
 2705: preserves the support hypergraph. Hence, if $C_k$ denotes the number of minimal
 2706: covers of a labelled $k$-set, then
 2707: \begin{equation}\label{eq:odd-minimal-cover-bound}
 2708:  \#\mathcal M_n\ge C_k,
 2709:  \qquad
 2710:  \widthop(B_n)\ge C_k.
 2711: \end{equation}
 2712: Through Royle's correspondence
 2713: \cite{Royle2000SetCoversSplitGraphs}, every isomorphism type of split graph on
 2714: $k$ vertices consequently has a rank-pure Fibonacci realization on the prime
 2715: coordinates of $n$.
 2716: \end{corollary}
 2717: 
 2718: \begin{proof}
 2719: Every nonempty $n_S$ is odd and at least $3$, so no support is exceptional.
 2720: Theorem~\ref{thm:rank-pure-universality} gives the embedding and support
 2721: preservation. Minimal generators form an antichain, which proves the width
 2722: bound. Royle's theorem is a correspondence between isomorphism types of split
 2723: graphs and minimal covers; choosing a labelling of a representative cover and
 2724: then applying the embedding gives the final assertion.
 2725: \end{proof}
 2726: 
 2727: We next fix the classical enumeration input, without claiming it as a result of
 2728: this paper. Let $C_{k,s}$ count the minimal covers of a labelled $k$-set having
 2729: $s$ members, put $C_k=\sum_s C_{k,s}$, and put
 2730: \[
 2731:  b_k:=\sum_{s=0}^k\binom{k}{s}2^{s(k-s)}.
 2732: \]
 2733: Hearne and Wagner \cite{HearneWagner1973MinimalCovers} proved
 2734: \begin{equation}\label{eq:hearne-wagner}
 2735:  C_{k,s}=\sum_{j=s}^k\binom{k}{j}
 2736:  \genfrac{\{}{\}}{0pt}{}{j}{s}
 2737:  (2^s-s-1)^{k-j}.
 2738: \end{equation}
 2739: The central-sum asymptotic occurring in the labelled split-graph enumeration of
 2740: Bender--Richmond--Wormald \cite{BenderRichmondWormald1985ChordalSplit}, in the
 2741: form recorded also by Troyka \cite[Section~3]{Troyka2019SplitGraphs}, gives,
 2742: with
 2743: \[
 2744:  \vartheta_0:=\sum_{r\in\mathbb Z}2^{-r^2},\qquad
 2745:  \vartheta_1:=\sum_{r\in\mathbb Z}2^{-(r+1/2)^2},\qquad
 2746:  \varepsilon\equiv k\pmod2,
 2747: \]
 2748: the published asymptotic
 2749: \begin{equation}\label{eq:minimal-cover-theta}
 2750:  C_k\sim b_k\sim
 2751:  \vartheta_\varepsilon\binom{k}{\lfloor k/2\rfloor}2^{k^2/4}.
 2752: \end{equation}
 2753: In particular, $b_{k-1}/b_k=O(2^{-k/2})$.
 2754: 
 2755: \begin{lemma}[Connected minimal-cover concentration]
 2756: \label{lem:connected-minimal-cover-concentration}
 2757: If $C_k^{\rm conn}$ denotes the number of connected minimal covers, then
 2758: \begin{equation}\label{eq:connected-minimal-cover-asymptotic}
 2759:  C_k-C_k^{\rm conn}=O(kC_{k-1})=o(C_k).
 2760: \end{equation}
 2761: \end{lemma}
 2762: 
 2763: \begin{proof}
 2764: Connected components of a minimal cover are themselves minimal covers, so the
 2765: labelled exponential formula gives
 2766: \[
 2767:  C_k=\sum_{j=1}^k\binom{k-1}{j-1}C_j^{\rm conn}C_{k-j}.
 2768: \]
 2769: The terms $j=1$ and $j=k-1$ contribute $O(kC_{k-1})$. Choosing the least
 2770: private element in each cover member gives $C_j\le b_j\le2^j2^{j^2/4}$.
 2771: For the remaining terms, use this bound and the central lower bound for
 2772: $C_{k-1}$ furnished by the $s=\lfloor(k-1)/2\rfloor$ term of
 2773: \eqref{eq:hearne-wagner}. After division by $kC_{k-1}$, the term indexed by
 2774: $j$ is
 2775: \[
 2776:  \ll \binom{k}{j}
 2777:  2^{-(j-1)(k-j-1)/2}\qquad(2\le j\le k-2).
 2778: \]
 2779: The sum of these bounds is bounded (and in fact tends to zero), by symmetry
 2780: and separation into $2\le j\le k/2$ and its reflection. This proves the first
 2781: estimate in \eqref{eq:connected-minimal-cover-asymptotic}; the second equality
 2782: follows from \eqref{eq:minimal-cover-theta}.
 2783: \end{proof}
 2784: 
 2785: \begin{corollary}[Theta-refined connected support entropy]
 2786: \label{cor:theta-refined-connected-entropy}
 2787: Uniformly along every sequence of integers for which $k=\omega(n)\to\infty$,
 2788: \begin{equation}\label{eq:theta-connected-lower-bound}
 2789:  \#\mathcal M_n^{\rm conn}\ge
 2790:  (1-o(1))\,
 2791:  \vartheta_\varepsilon\binom{k}{\lfloor k/2\rfloor}2^{k^2/4},
 2792:  \qquad \varepsilon\equiv k\pmod2.
 2793: \end{equation}
 2794: Consequently
 2795: \begin{equation}\label{eq:theta-connected-log-lower-bound}
 2796:  \log\#\mathcal M_n^{\rm conn}\ge

 succeeded in 472ms:
 2797:  \frac{\log2}{4}k^2+k\log2-\frac12\log k
 2798:  +\log\vartheta_\varepsilon+\frac12\log\frac2\pi+o(1).
 2799: \end{equation}
 2800: The same lower bounds hold with $\mathcal M_n$ in place of
 2801: $\mathcal M_n^{\rm conn}$.
 2802: \end{corollary}
 2803: 
 2804: \begin{proof}
 2805: Fix an exceptional support $S_0\in\mathcal E_n$. If a minimal cover contains
 2806: $S_0$, choose a canonical private vertex $x\in S_0$ of that member. Every
 2807: other member avoids $x$ and is recovered from its canonical private vertex and
 2808: an arbitrary subset of the remaining nonprivate vertices. The number of such
 2809: encodings is at most $b_{k-1}$ for each choice of $x$. Therefore the number of
 2810: minimal covers meeting $\mathcal E_n$ is at most
 2811: \[
 2812:  \sum_{S_0\in\mathcal E_n}|S_0|b_{k-1}\le4b_{k-1}=o(C_k).
 2813: \]
 2814: Lemma~\ref{lem:connected-minimal-cover-concentration} also shows that only
 2815: $o(C_k)$ minimal covers are disconnected. Thus $(1-o(1))C_k$ covers are simultaneously
 2816: connected and disjoint from $\mathcal E_n$. Theorem
 2817: \ref{thm:rank-pure-universality} embeds them in
 2818: $\mathcal M_n^{\rm conn}$, proving \eqref{eq:theta-connected-lower-bound}.
 2819: Taking logarithms and using
 2820: \[
 2821:  \log\binom{k}{\lfloor k/2\rfloor}
 2822:  =k\log2-\frac12\log k+\frac12\log\frac2\pi+o(1)
 2823: \]
 2824: gives \eqref{eq:theta-connected-log-lower-bound}.
 2825: \end{proof}
 2826: 
 2827: \begin{corollary}[Discrete local limit in a fixed-prime rank-pure sector]
 2828: \label{cor:rank-pure-local-limit}
 2829: Let $(n_j)$ be any sequence with $k_j=\omega(n_j)\to\infty$. For every
 2830: $j$ and every nonexceptional nonempty support
 2831: $S\subseteq\mathcal P(n_j)$, fix one prime
 2832: $\pi_{j,S}\in\Pi_\alpha((n_j)_S)$. Let $\mathcal Q_{n_j}^{\rm rp}$ consist of the
 2833: products $\prod_{S\in\mathcal C}\pi_{j,S}$ over all
 2834: $\mathcal C\in\operatorname{MinCov}(\mathcal P(n_j))$ disjoint from
 2835: $\mathcal E_{n_j}$, and choose $m$ uniformly from $\mathcal Q_{n_j}^{\rm rp}$. Put
 2836: $W_j=\omega(m)$. Along the subsequence $k_j=2q_j$, for every fixed
 2837: $d\in\mathbb Z$,
 2838: \begin{equation}\label{eq:even-rank-pure-local-limit}
 2839:  \Pr(W_j=q_j+d)\longrightarrow\frac{2^{-d^2}}{\vartheta_0}.
 2840: \end{equation}
 2841: Along the subsequence $k_j=2q_j+1$,
 2842: \begin{equation}\label{eq:odd-rank-pure-local-limit}
 2843:  \Pr(W_j=q_j+d)\longrightarrow
 2844:  \frac{2^{-(d-1/2)^2}}{\vartheta_1}.
 2845: \end{equation}
 2846: In particular, $W_j=k_j/2+O_{\mathbb P}(1)$.
 2847: \end{corollary}
 2848: 
 2849: \begin{proof}
 2850: Unique factorization and distinct support ranks show that $W_j$ is the number
 2851: of members of the corresponding cover. By the proof of
 2852: Corollary~\ref{cor:theta-refined-connected-entropy}, deleting covers meeting
 2853: $\mathcal E_{n_j}$ changes the uniform distribution in total variation by
 2854: $o(1)$. For $s=\lfloor k/2\rfloor+d$ with fixed $d$, the $j=s$ term of
 2855: \eqref{eq:hearne-wagner} and the canonical-private-element encoding give
 2856: \[
 2857:  \binom{k}{s}(2^s-s-1)^{k-s}
 2858:  \le C_{k,s}\le \binom{k}{s}2^{s(k-s)},
 2859:  \qquad
 2860:  C_{k,s}\sim\binom{k}{s}2^{s(k-s)}.
 2861: \]
 2862: Dividing this by \eqref{eq:minimal-cover-theta} gives
 2863: \eqref{eq:even-rank-pure-local-limit} and
 2864: \eqref{eq:odd-rank-pure-local-limit}. The quadratic limiting tails are
 2865: summable, which is equivalent to tightness on the integer scale.
 2866: \end{proof}
 2867: 
 2868: \begin{remark}\label{rem:connected-lower-not-dominance}
 2869: Corollary~\ref{cor:theta-refined-connected-entropy} proves that connected
 2870: minimal generators alone realize the full currently known lower support
 2871: entropy, including its linear and logarithmic corrections. It does not compare
 2872: the total connected and disconnected parts of $\mathcal M_n$. Such a comparison
 2873: would require upper control of the arithmetic multiplicities in $R(n)$ and is
 2874: not presently available.
 2875: \end{remark}
 2876: 
 2877: \begin{theorem}[Combinatorial support entropy]
 2878: \label{thm:minimal-generator-growth}
 2879: For every integer $n\ge3$, put $k=\omega(n)$ and
 2880: $L(n)=\log\#\mathcal M_n$. Then $\mathcal M_n$ is nonempty. If $k\ge3$ and
 2881: $r=\lfloor k/2\rfloor$, then
 2882: \begin{equation}\label{eq:private-cover-finite-bounds}
 2883:  (2^r-1)^{k-r}
 2884:  \le \#\mathcal M_n
 2885:  \le \sum_{s=1}^{k}\binom{k}{s}(2R(n))^s2^{s(k-s)}.
 2886: \end{equation}
 2887: In particular, uniformly along every sequence for which $\omega(n)\to\infty$,
 2888: \begin{equation}\label{eq:private-cover-entropy-order}
 2889:  \frac{\log2}{4}\,\omega(n)^2-O(1)
 2890:  \le L(n)
 2891:  \le \frac{\log2}{4}\,\omega(n)^2
 2892:       +\omega(n)\log R(n)+O(\omega(n)).
 2893: \end{equation}
 2894: The coefficient $\log2/4$ is the exact entropy coefficient for the support
 2895: combinatorics. This is not an asymptotic formula for $L(n)$ unless
 2896: $R(n)=\exp(o(\omega(n)))$; no such unconditional estimate for $R(n)$ is
 2897: proved here.
 2898: 
 2899: For comparison, the crude support-count estimate gives
 2900: \begin{equation}
 2901: \label{eq:finite-minimal-generator-bound}
 2902:  \#\mathcal M_n
 2903:  \le \sum_{r=0}^{k}\binom{\Omega(F_n)}{r}
 2904:  \le n^k.
 2905: \end{equation}
 2906: Combining only the last inequality with the classical maximal order of
 2907: $\omega(n)$ gives the crude corollary
 2908: \begin{equation}
 2909: \label{eq:crude-maximal-order}
 2910:  \limsup_{n\to\infty}
 2911:  \frac{L(n)\log\log n}{(\log n)^2}\le1.
 2912: \end{equation}
 2913: This envelope has scale $(\log n)^2/\log\log n$, a factor $\log\log n$
 2914: above the witness-cover entropy scale; it is not a consequence of
 2915: \eqref{eq:private-cover-entropy-order}.
 2916: At the coarser divisor-lattice scale, the antichain property and Wigert's
 2917: theorem give the Sperner-type envelope
 2918: \begin{equation}
 2919: \label{eq:sperner-wigert-envelope}
 2920:  L(n)\le\log\tau(F_n)
 2921:  \le (\log2\,\log\varphi+o(1))\frac{n}{\log n},
 2922:  \qquad \varphi=\frac{1+\sqrt5}{2};
 2923: \end{equation}
 2924: in fact \eqref{eq:crude-maximal-order} is stronger and implies
 2925: $L(n)=o(n/\log n)$.
 2926: 
 2927: Moreover, as $x\to\infty$,
 2928: \begin{equation}
 2929: \label{eq:mean-logarithmic-growth}
 2930:  \left(\frac{\log2}{4}+o(1)\right)x(\log\log x)^2
 2931:  \le \sum_{3\le n\le x}L(n)
 2932:  \le (1+o(1))x\log x\,\log\log x.
 2933: \end{equation}
 2934: 
 2935: \end{theorem}
 2936: 
 2937: \begin{proof}
 2938: First, $F_n\in B_n$ for every $n\ge3$.  Indeed,
 2939: $F_n\mid F_d$ implies $F_n\le F_d$, and strict increase of the Fibonacci
 2940: sequence from index $2$ onward forces $d\ge n$; hence $\alpha(F_n)=n$.
 2941: Because $B_n$ is a nonempty finite poset, it has a minimal element, so
 2942: $\mathcal M_n\ne\varnothing$ and $L(n)$ is defined.
 2943: 
 2944: We first prove the explicit finite private-cover bound in
 2945: \eqref{eq:private-cover-finite-bounds}. Fix $k\ge3$, and give
 2946: $\mathcal P(n)$ an arbitrary order. Put
 2947: \[
 2948:  X=\mathcal P(n)\cap\{2,3\},\qquad r=\lfloor k/2\rfloor.
 2949: \]
 2950: Since $k-\#X\ge k-2\ge r$, choose an $r$-element set
 2951: $P\subseteq\mathcal P(n)\setminus X$, and put
 2952: $Q=\mathcal P(n)\setminus P$. For every map
 2953: \[
 2954:  H:Q\longrightarrow 2^P\setminus\{\varnothing\}
 2955: \]
 2956: and every $i\in P$, define
 2957: \[
 2958:  T_i=\{i\}\cup\{q\in Q:i\in H(q)\}.
 2959: \]
 2960: The index $n_{T_i}=\prod_{\ell\in T_i}\ell^{\nu_\ell(n)}$ has a prime
 2961: divisor outside $\{2,3\}$, because $i\in P$. Hence $n_{T_i}\ge5$ and
 2962: $n_{T_i}\notin\{6,12\}$. Theorem~\ref{thm:primitive-divisors} supplies a
 2963: prime $\pi_{T_i}$ with $\alpha(\pi_{T_i})=n_{T_i}$; fix one such prime for
 2964: each support that occurs.
 2965: 
 2966: The supports $T_i$ cover $\mathcal P(n)$ because $H(q)$ is nonempty for every
 2967: $q\in Q$. Moreover, $i$ belongs to $T_i$ and to no $T_j$ with $j\ne i$.
 2968: Each $\pi_{T_i}$ is a prime atom, so
 2969: $E_n(\pi_{T_i})=T_n(\pi_{T_i})=T_i$. Thus
 2970: $\{\pi_{T_i}:i\in P\}$ is an $n$-witness cover. Distinct supports have
 2971: distinct ranks and therefore distinct underlying primes. If two maps $H$
 2972: differ, then their support families differ, since the edge containing the
 2973: private coordinate $i$ identifies $T_i$; unique factorization then gives
 2974: different minimal generators. There are
 2975: $(2^r-1)^{\#Q}=(2^r-1)^{k-r}$ maps $H$, proving the lower bound in
 2976: \eqref{eq:private-cover-finite-bounds}.
 2977: 
 2978: For the upper bound, let $\Theta$ be an $n$-witness cover with $s$ atoms. For
 2979: each $\theta\in\Theta$, choose the least coordinate
 2980: \[
 2981:  x_\theta\in E_n(\theta)\setminus
 2982:  \bigcup_{\eta\in\Theta\setminus\{\theta\}}T_n(\eta).
 2983: \]
 2984: These private coordinates are distinct; write
 2985: $P=\{x_\theta:\theta\in\Theta\}$ and
 2986: $Q=\mathcal P(n)\setminus P$. If $x_\theta=x$, then
 2987: \[
 2988:  T_n(\theta)=\{x\}\cup U_\theta,\qquad U_\theta\subseteq Q,
 2989: \]
 2990: because every other member of $P$ is private to another atom. By
 2991: Lemma~\ref{thm:primitive-ladder-dichotomy}, either
 2992: $E_n(\theta)=T_n(\theta)$ or $E_n(\theta)=\{x\}$. Hence there are at most two
 2993: support-pair alternatives, and the definition
 2994: \eqref{eq:rank-window-multiplicity} allows at most $R(n)$ atoms for each.
 2995: Thus, once $P$ is fixed, the atom indexed by each
 2996: $x\in P$ has at most $2R(n)2^{k-s}$ choices. Ignoring coverage and
 2997: coprimeness only enlarges the count, so summing over the
 2998: $\binom{k}{s}$ possible sets $P$ and over $1\le s\le k$ proves the upper
 2999: bound in \eqref{eq:private-cover-finite-bounds}.
 3000: 
 3001: Now $r(k-r)=\lfloor k^2/4\rfloor$ and
 3002: $(k-r)\log(1-2^{-r})=O(1)$. Hence the logarithm of the lower bound is
 3003: $(\log2)k^2/4+O(1)$. For the upper bound, use
 3004: $s(k-s)\le k^2/4$, $s\le k$, and
 3005: $\sum_s\binom{k}{s}\le2^k$ to obtain
 3006: \[
 3007:  \log\sum_{s=1}^{k}\binom{k}{s}(2R(n))^s2^{s(k-s)}
 3008:  \le\frac{\log2}{4}k^2+k\log R(n)+O(k).
 3009: \]
 3010: This proves \eqref{eq:private-cover-entropy-order}.
 3011: 
 3012: Let
 3013: \[
 3014:  \mathcal Q_n=\{p^e:p\mid F_n,\ 1\le e\le\nu_p(F_n)\}.
 3015: \]
 3016: This set has cardinality $\Omega(F_n)$.  By unique factorization, the map
 3017: \[
 3018:  m\longmapsto\{p^{\nu_p(m)}:p\mid m\}
 3019: \]
 3020: is injective from $\mathcal M_n$ into the subsets of $\mathcal Q_n$.
 3021: Corollary~\ref{cor:support-bound} says that every image has at most
 3022: $k=\omega(n)$ members.  This proves the first inequality in
 3023: \eqref{eq:finite-minimal-generator-bound}.
 3024: 
 3025: Since every prime factor of $F_n$ is at least $2$,
 3026: $2^{\Omega(F_n)}\le F_n$.  Also $F_n<2^{n-1}$ for $n\ge3$, by the elementary
 3027: induction $F_{j+1}=F_j+F_{j-1}<2^j$.  Hence
 3028: $\Omega(F_n)\le n-2$.  A subset of an $N$-element set having at most $k$
 3029: members can be encoded injectively by its increasing list padded to length
 3030: $k$ with a new symbol; therefore
 3031: \[
 3032:  \sum_{r=0}^k\binom Nr\le(N+1)^k.
 3033: \]
 3034: Taking $N=\Omega(F_n)$ gives the second inequality in
 3035: \eqref{eq:finite-minimal-generator-bound}.
 3036: 
 3037: The classical maximal-order formula for $\omega(n)$
 3038: \cite[Theorem~22.11]{HardyWright2008Introduction}
 3039: \[
 3040:  \limsup_{n\to\infty}
 3041:  \frac{\omega(n)\log\log n}{\log n}=1
 3042: \]
 3043: now combines with $L(n)\le\omega(n)\log n$ to prove
 3044: \eqref{eq:crude-maximal-order}. This argument uses only the crude estimate
 3045: $\#\mathcal M_n\le n^{\omega(n)}$, not the witness-cover entropy bound. On
 3046: the other hand,
 3047: $\mathcal M_n$ is an antichain in the divisor lattice of $F_n$, so
 3048: \[
 3049:  \#\mathcal M_n\le\widthop(\operatorname{Div}(F_n))\le\tau(F_n).
 3050: \]
 3051: Wigert's maximal-order theorem \cite{Wigert1907Divisors} states that
 3052: \[
 3053:  \log\tau(N)\le(\log2+o(1))\frac{\log N}{\log\log N}.
 3054: \]
 3055: Binet's formula gives $\log F_n=n\log\varphi+O(1)$ and
 3056: $\log\log F_n=\log n+O(1)$, yielding
 3057: \eqref{eq:sperner-wigert-envelope}.  Finally,
 3058: $(\log n)^2/\log\log n=o(n/\log n)$, so
 3059: \eqref{eq:crude-maximal-order} gives the stated strict improvement.
 3060: 
 3061: For the mean upper bound, use the classical mean-value estimate
 3062: \cite{HardyRamanujan1917NormalPrimeFactors}
 3063: \begin{equation}
 3064: \label{eq:mean-omega-input}
 3065:  \sum_{n\le x}\omega(n)=x\log\log x+O(x),
 3066: \end{equation}
 3067: together with $L(n)\le\omega(n)\log n$.  Uniformly for $n\le x$,
 3068: $\log n\le\log x$, and hence
 3069: \[
 3070:  \sum_{3\le n\le x}L(n)
 3071:  \le\log x\sum_{n\le x}\omega(n)
 3072:  =(1+o(1))x\log x\,\log\log x.
 3073: \]
 3074: 
 3075: Tur\'an's second-moment form of the Hardy--Ramanujan theorem
 3076: \cite{Turan1934HardyRamanujan} gives

 succeeded in 328ms:
 3077: \begin{equation}\label{eq:omega-second-moment}
 3078:  \sum_{n\le x}\omega(n)^2=(1+o(1))x(\log\log x)^2.
 3079: \end{equation}
 3080: After enlarging the absolute constant in the lower half of
 3081: \eqref{eq:private-cover-entropy-order}, it holds as
 3082: $L(n)\ge(\log2)\omega(n)^2/4-O(1)$ also for $\omega(n)<3$.
 3083: Summation and \eqref{eq:omega-second-moment} prove the lower half of
 3084: \eqref{eq:mean-logarithmic-growth} and complete the proof.
 3085: \end{proof}
 3086: 
 3087: \begin{lemma}[Known baseline for the arithmetic interface]
 3088: \label{lem:R-baseline}
 3089: For every $n\ge3$,
 3090: \[
 3091:  R(n)\le 1+\omega(F_n)=O(n),
 3092:  \qquad
 3093:  \log R(n)\le\log n+O(1).
 3094: \]
 3095: Moreover, Stroi\'nski's cumulative estimate gives
 3096: \[
 3097:  \limsup_{x\to\infty}\frac{\log x}{x^2}
 3098:  \#\{p:\alpha(p)\le x\}
 3099:  \le \frac{3\log\varphi}{2\pi^2}.
 3100: \]
 3101: Neither estimate implies the conjectures below.
 3102: \end{lemma}
 3103: 
 3104: \begin{proof}
 3105: Every prime atom in a family $\mathcal A_{I,J}(n)$ divides $F_n$, and a fixed
 3106: ladder family contains at most one atom. Thus a family contains at most all
 3107: distinct prime divisors of $F_n$ together with one ladder atom, proving
 3108: $R(n)\le1+\omega(F_n)$. Since
 3109: $2^{\omega(F_n)}\le\operatorname{rad}(F_n)\le F_n$ and
 3110: $\log F_n=n\log\varphi+O(1)$, this gives $R(n)=O(n)$ and the logarithmic
 3111: bound. The cumulative estimate is Stroi\'nski's Theorem~7
 3112: \cite{Stroinski2016AlphaContraction}.
 3113: 
 3114: Quantitatively, the normal-order conjecture below asks for
 3115: $\log R(n)=o(\log\log n)$ for almost all $n$, whereas the elementary estimate
 3116: gives only $O(\log n)$. The cumulative estimate controls all primes of rank at
 3117: most $x$ together and likewise gives at best a logarithmic bound of order
 3118: $\log n$ for a single window. For the mean conjecture, the elementary bound
 3119: and the mean order of $\omega$ give only
 3120: $O(x\log x\log\log x)$, not $o(x(\log\log x)^2)$.
 3121: \end{proof}
 3122: 
 3123: \begin{remark}[Cumulative rank estimates do not imply \textup{(H2)}]
 3124: \label{rem:cumulative-rank-estimates-insufficient}
 3125: This nonimplication can be made quantitative without asserting that the model
 3126: below is realized by Fibonacci ranks. Fix $0<c<\log\varphi/2$ and put
 3127: \[
 3128:  a_0(d):=\max\left\{1,
 3129:  \left\lfloor\frac{c\phi_{\rm E}(d)}{\log(2d)}\right\rfloor\right\}.
 3130: \]
 3131: Elementary integral comparison gives
 3132: \[
 3133:  \sum_{d\le y}a_0(d)\ll\frac{y^2}{\log y},\qquad
 3134:  a_0(d)\ll1+\frac{\phi_{\rm E}(d)}{\log(2d)},\qquad
 3135:  \sum_{d\le y}a_0(d)\log d\ll y^2.
 3136: \]
 3137: Thus this formal multiplicity sequence has the cumulative, pointwise, and
 3138: logarithmic-mass scales supplied by the presently known estimates, including
 3139: Theorem~\ref{thm:fibotomic-rank-entropy}. On the other hand, the standard
 3140: lower bound for Euler's function gives
 3141: \[
 3142:  \log a_0(n)=\log n-\log\log n+O(\log\log\log(3n))
 3143: \]
 3144: for large $n$.
 3145: Partial summation in \eqref{eq:mean-omega-input} therefore gives
 3146: \[
 3147:  \sum_{n\le x}\omega(n)\log a_0(n)
 3148:  \sim x\log x\log\log x,
 3149: \]
 3150: which is larger than the scale in \textup{(H2)}. Hence cumulative exact-rank
 3151: counts alone cannot establish the required visible-divisor maximum estimate.
 3152: \end{remark}
 3153: 
 3154: \begin{corollary}[Unconditional lower growth]
 3155: \label{cor:unconditional-lower-growth}
 3156: For almost all $n$,
 3157: \[
 3158:  \log\#\mathcal M_n\ge
 3159:  \left(\frac{\log2}{4}+o(1)\right)(\log\log n)^2.
 3160: \]
 3161: If $N_y:=\prod_{p\le y}p$, then
 3162: \[
 3163:  \log\#\mathcal M_{N_y}\ge
 3164:  \left(\frac{\log2}{4}+o(1)\right)
 3165:  \frac{(\log N_y)^2}{(\log\log N_y)^2}.
 3166: \]
 3167: \end{corollary}
 3168: 
 3169: \begin{proof}
 3170: The Hardy--Ramanujan normal-order theorem states that
 3171: $\omega(n)=(1+o(1))\log\log n$ for almost all $n$
 3172: \cite{HardyRamanujan1917NormalPrimeFactors,%
 3173: Tenenbaum2015AnalyticProbabilistic}. Substitution in the lower half of
 3174: \eqref{eq:private-cover-entropy-order} proves the first assertion. The prime
 3175: number theorem gives
 3176: $\omega(N_y)=\pi(y)\sim y/\log y\sim\log N_y/\log\log N_y$; substituting this
 3177: in the same lower bound proves the second assertion.
 3178: \end{proof}
 3179: 
 3180: \begin{conjecture}[Normal rank-window sparsity]\label{hyp:normal-window}
 3181: For every $\varepsilon>0$,
 3182: \[
 3183:  \#\{n\le x:\log R(n)>\varepsilon\omega(n)\}=o(x).
 3184: \tag{H1}
 3185: \]
 3186: \end{conjecture}
 3187: 
 3188: \begin{conjecture}[Mean sparse-window hypothesis]\label{hyp:mean-window}
 3189: One has
 3190: \[
 3191:  \sum_{3\le n\le x}\omega(n)\log R(n)
 3192:  =o\bigl(x(\log\log x)^2\bigr).
 3193: \tag{H2}
 3194: \]
 3195: \end{conjecture}
 3196: 
 3197: \begin{proposition}[Conditional obstruction from Fibonacci prime divisors]
 3198: \label{prop:blms-obstructs-mean-window}
 3199: Assume Conjecture~5.1 of Bugeaud--Luca--Mignotte--Siksek
 3200: \cite{BugeaudLucaMignotteSiksek2005FewPrimeDivisors}, namely that
 3201: $\omega(F_n)\gg\log n$ for composite $n$. Then
 3202: \begin{equation}\label{eq:blms-mean-window-obstruction}
 3203:  \liminf_{x\to\infty}
 3204:  \frac{\sum_{3\le n\le x}\omega(n)\log R(n)}
 3205:  {x(\log\log x)^2}
 3206:  \ge\frac6{\pi^2}(1-\log2)>0.
 3207: \end{equation}
 3208: In particular, under that published conjecture, the mean sparse-window
 3209: hypothesis \textup{(H2)} is false.
 3210: \end{proposition}
 3211: 
 3212: \begin{proof}
 3213: Let $n$ be squarefree and put $k=\omega(n)$. The exact-rank partition of the
 3214: prime divisors of $F_n$ gives
 3215: \[
 3216:  \omega(F_n)=\sum_{d\mid n}a(d).
 3217: \]
 3218: For squarefree $n$, every nonempty support $J\subseteq\mathcal P(n)$ has the
 3219: single rank $d=\prod_{\ell\in J}\ell$ in its window. Since $a(1)=0$, the
 3220: pigeonhole principle and \eqref{eq:prime-window-versus-R} give
 3221: \begin{equation}\label{eq:squarefree-blms-pigeonhole}
 3222:  R(n)\ge\max_{d\mid n}a(d)
 3223:  \ge\frac{\omega(F_n)}{2^k-1}.
 3224: \end{equation}
 3225: Under the stated conjecture, for every sufficiently large squarefree composite
 3226: $n$,
 3227: \[
 3228:  \log R(n)\ge\log\log n-k\log2+O(1).
 3229: \]
 3230: The Hardy--Ramanujan theorem has an exceptional set of $o(x)$ integers, so on
 3231: all but $o(x)$ squarefree integers one has
 3232: $k=(1+o(1))\log\log n$. Consequently
 3233: \[
 3234:  \omega(n)\log R(n)
 3235:  \ge(1-\log2+o(1))(\log\log n)^2
 3236: \]
 3237: on this set. Squarefree integers have density $6/\pi^2$, and squarefree prime
 3238: indices have density zero. Summation over squarefree composite indices proves
 3239: \eqref{eq:blms-mean-window-obstruction}.
 3240: \end{proof}
 3241: 
 3242: \begin{corollary}[Conditional normal and mean orders]
 3243: \label{cor:conditional-orders}
 3244: Assuming Conjecture~\ref{hyp:normal-window},
 3245: \begin{equation}\label{eq:conditional-normal-order}
 3246:  L(n)\sim\frac{\log2}{4}(\log\log n)^2
 3247:  \quad\text{for almost all }n.
 3248: \end{equation}
 3249: Assuming Conjecture~\ref{hyp:mean-window},
 3250: \begin{equation}\label{eq:conditional-mean-order}
 3251:  \sum_{3\le n\le x}L(n)
 3252:  \sim\frac{\log2}{4}x(\log\log x)^2.
 3253: \end{equation}
 3254: Neither conclusion is unconditional.
 3255: \end{corollary}
 3256: 
 3257: \begin{proof}
 3258: For the first assertion, intersect the density-one set in
 3259: Conjecture~\ref{hyp:normal-window} with the Hardy--Ramanujan normal-order set.
 3260: Then the error $\omega(n)\log R(n)+O(\omega(n))$ in
 3261: \eqref{eq:private-cover-entropy-order} is $o(\omega(n)^2)$. For the second,
 3262: sum the upper bound in \eqref{eq:private-cover-entropy-order}, use
 3263: Conjecture~\ref{hyp:mean-window}, \eqref{eq:omega-second-moment}, and
 3264: $\sum_{n\le x}\omega(n)=O(x\log\log x)$, and compare with the unconditional
 3265: lower mean bound \eqref{eq:mean-logarithmic-growth}.
 3266: \end{proof}
 3267: 
 3268: \begin{remark}[Exact residual arithmetic interface]
 3269: \label{rem:exact-average-open-interface}
 3270: Corollary~\ref{cor:theta-refined-connected-entropy} and
 3271: Theorem~\ref{thm:minimal-generator-growth} determine the currently available
 3272: support contribution: $\log2/4$ is its exact quadratic coefficient, the
 3273: rank-pure sector supplies its theta-refined lower terms, and the unresolved
 3274: term in the upper bound is $\omega(n)\log R(n)$. The rank partition used in
 3275: Lemma~\ref{lem:three-coordinate-arithmetic-tests} applies verbatim to every
 3276: support size: prime-atom families are finite disjoint unions of the rank
 3277: classes $\Pi_\alpha(d)$, while ladder families are singletons. Thus
 3278: Conjectures~\ref{hyp:normal-window} and
 3279: \ref{hyp:mean-window} ask precisely for upper distributional control of
 3280: exact-rank prime multiplicities in those rank windows.
 3281: 
 3282: The pointwise estimate \eqref{eq:pointwise-exact-rank-bound} gives new control
 3283: of each individual class, but
 3284: Remark~\ref{rem:cumulative-rank-estimates-insufficient} shows that even its
 3285: Euler-factor scale does not by itself imply either rank-window conjecture. In
 3286: particular, no optimality claim about all possible uses of fibotomic
 3287: information is needed here.
 3288: 
 3289: The primitive-divisor theorems of Carmichael and Bilu--Hanrot--Voutier, and
 3290: the refinements and size results of Kiss, Granville, and Hong
 3291: \cite{Kiss1988PrimitiveDivisors,Granville2012PrimitivePrimeFactors,%
 3292: Hong2025BigPrimitiveDivisors}, give existence or information about the size of
 3293: primitive divisors, not the required upper multiplicity estimates. The
 3294: Hardy--Ramanujan and
 3295: Tur\'an theorems control the additive function $\omega(n)$ but not the prime
 3296: factorization of $F_d$ across exact apparition ranks. Likewise, the
 3297: Erd\H{o}s--Wintner theorem \cite{ErdosWintner1939Additive} concerns limiting
 3298: distributions of additive arithmetic functions, whereas $L(n)$ and $R(n)$
 3299: are not additive. No implication from RH or ERH to either rank-window
 3300: conjecture is presently known. Accordingly, the normal and mean equivalents
 3301: \eqref{eq:conditional-normal-order} and
 3302: \eqref{eq:conditional-mean-order} are conditional; the unconditional
 3303: statements are \eqref{eq:private-cover-entropy-order} and
 3304: \eqref{eq:mean-logarithmic-growth}, together with the stronger connected lower
 3305: bound \eqref{eq:theta-connected-log-lower-bound}. None of these estimates
 3306: implies that the disconnected part of $\mathcal M_n$ is negligible; the
 3307: strictly stronger blockwise hypothesis needed for that conclusion is isolated
 3308: in Theorem~\ref{thm:blockwise-connected-dominance} below.
 3309: \end{remark}
 3310: 
 3311: \begin{lemma}[Refined private-coordinate upper bound]
 3312: \label{lem:refined-private-coordinate-upper-bound}
 3313: For $m\ge2$, put $s=\omega(m)$ and $r=R(m)$. Then
 3314: \begin{equation}\label{eq:refined-private-coordinate-upper-bound}
 3315:  \#\mathcal M_m\le U_s(r),\qquad
 3316:  U_s(r):=\sum_{t=1}^s\binom st
 3317:              \bigl(r2^{s-t}+1\bigr)^t.
 3318: \end{equation}
 3319: There is an absolute constant $K$ such that, for all $s,r\ge1$,
 3320: \begin{equation}\label{eq:refined-upper-versus-covers}
 3321:  U_s(r)\le Kr^sC_s.
 3322: \end{equation}
 3323: \end{lemma}
 3324: 
 3325: \begin{proof}
 3326: The case $m=2$ is immediate from $\mathcal M_2=\varnothing$ and $R(2)=1$.
 3327: Suppose $m\ge3$, and let a witness cover have $t$ atoms. Choose the canonical
 3328: private coordinate of each atom as in the proof of
 3329: Theorem~\ref{thm:minimal-generator-growth}; let $P$ be the resulting
 3330: $t$-set and $Q$ its complement. For a fixed $x\in P$, a prime atom has full
 3331: support $\{x\}\cup U$ for some $U\subseteq Q$, giving at most
 3332: $r2^{s-t}$ choices. A ladder atom with essential coordinate $x$ must be a
 3333: power of the index prime $x$, and the full-exponent equation in
 3334: Proposition~\ref{prop:prime-power-ladders} determines at most one such atom
 3335: over all its possible full supports. Thus the atom assigned to $x$ has at most
 3336: $r2^{s-t}+1$ choices. Summing over $P$ and $t$ proves

 2876: 
 2877: \begin{theorem}[Combinatorial support entropy]
 2878: \label{thm:minimal-generator-growth}
 2879: For every integer $n\ge3$, put $k=\omega(n)$ and
 2880: $L(n)=\log\#\mathcal M_n$. Then $\mathcal M_n$ is nonempty. If $k\ge3$ and
 2881: $r=\lfloor k/2\rfloor$, then
 2882: \begin{equation}\label{eq:private-cover-finite-bounds}
 2883:  (2^r-1)^{k-r}
 2884:  \le \#\mathcal M_n
 2885:  \le \sum_{s=1}^{k}\binom{k}{s}(2R(n))^s2^{s(k-s)}.
 2886: \end{equation}
 2887: In particular, uniformly along every sequence for which $\omega(n)\to\infty$,
 2888: \begin{equation}\label{eq:private-cover-entropy-order}
 2889:  \frac{\log2}{4}\,\omega(n)^2-O(1)
 2890:  \le L(n)
 2891:  \le \frac{\log2}{4}\,\omega(n)^2
 2892:       +\omega(n)\log R(n)+O(\omega(n)).
 2893: \end{equation}
 2894: The coefficient $\log2/4$ is the exact entropy coefficient for the support
 2895: combinatorics. This is not an asymptotic formula for $L(n)$ unless
 2896: $R(n)=\exp(o(\omega(n)))$; no such unconditional estimate for $R(n)$ is
 2897: proved here.
 2898: 
 2899: For comparison, the crude support-count estimate gives
 2900: \begin{equation}
 2901: \label{eq:finite-minimal-generator-bound}
 2902:  \#\mathcal M_n
 2903:  \le \sum_{r=0}^{k}\binom{\Omega(F_n)}{r}
 2904:  \le n^k.
 2905: \end{equation}
 2906: Combining only the last inequality with the classical maximal order of
 2907: $\omega(n)$ gives the crude corollary
 2908: \begin{equation}
 2909: \label{eq:crude-maximal-order}
 2910:  \limsup_{n\to\infty}
 2911:  \frac{L(n)\log\log n}{(\log n)^2}\le1.
 2912: \end{equation}
 2913: This envelope has scale $(\log n)^2/\log\log n$, a factor $\log\log n$
 2914: above the witness-cover entropy scale; it is not a consequence of
 2915: \eqref{eq:private-cover-entropy-order}.
 2916: At the coarser divisor-lattice scale, the antichain property and Wigert's
 2917: theorem give the Sperner-type envelope
 2918: \begin{equation}
 2919: \label{eq:sperner-wigert-envelope}
 2920:  L(n)\le\log\tau(F_n)
 2921:  \le (\log2\,\log\varphi+o(1))\frac{n}{\log n},
 2922:  \qquad \varphi=\frac{1+\sqrt5}{2};
 2923: \end{equation}
 2924: in fact \eqref{eq:crude-maximal-order} is stronger and implies
 2925: $L(n)=o(n/\log n)$.
 2926: 
 2927: Moreover, as $x\to\infty$,
 2928: \begin{equation}
 2929: \label{eq:mean-logarithmic-growth}
 2930:  \left(\frac{\log2}{4}+o(1)\right)x(\log\log x)^2
 2931:  \le \sum_{3\le n\le x}L(n)
 2932:  \le (1+o(1))x\log x\,\log\log x.
 2933: \end{equation}
 2934: 
 2935: \end{theorem}
 2936: 
 2937: \begin{proof}
 2938: First, $F_n\in B_n$ for every $n\ge3$.  Indeed,
 2939: $F_n\mid F_d$ implies $F_n\le F_d$, and strict increase of the Fibonacci
 2940: sequence from index $2$ onward forces $d\ge n$; hence $\alpha(F_n)=n$.
 2941: Because $B_n$ is a nonempty finite poset, it has a minimal element, so
 2942: $\mathcal M_n\ne\varnothing$ and $L(n)$ is defined.
 2943: 
 2944: We first prove the explicit finite private-cover bound in
 2945: \eqref{eq:private-cover-finite-bounds}. Fix $k\ge3$, and give
 2946: $\mathcal P(n)$ an arbitrary order. Put
 2947: \[
 2948:  X=\mathcal P(n)\cap\{2,3\},\qquad r=\lfloor k/2\rfloor.
 2949: \]
 2950: Since $k-\#X\ge k-2\ge r$, choose an $r$-element set
 2951: $P\subseteq\mathcal P(n)\setminus X$, and put
 2952: $Q=\mathcal P(n)\setminus P$. For every map
 2953: \[
 2954:  H:Q\longrightarrow 2^P\setminus\{\varnothing\}
 2955: \]
 2956: and every $i\in P$, define
 2957: \[
 2958:  T_i=\{i\}\cup\{q\in Q:i\in H(q)\}.
 2959: \]
 2960: The index $n_{T_i}=\prod_{\ell\in T_i}\ell^{\nu_\ell(n)}$ has a prime
 2961: divisor outside $\{2,3\}$, because $i\in P$. Hence $n_{T_i}\ge5$ and
 2962: $n_{T_i}\notin\{6,12\}$. Theorem~\ref{thm:primitive-divisors} supplies a
 2963: prime $\pi_{T_i}$ with $\alpha(\pi_{T_i})=n_{T_i}$; fix one such prime for
 2964: each support that occurs.
 2965: 
 2966: The supports $T_i$ cover $\mathcal P(n)$ because $H(q)$ is nonempty for every

 2691: \]
 2692: so Definition~\ref{def:support-hypergraph} gives precisely the edge set
 2693: $\mathcal C$ and therefore preserves connected components.
 2694: \end{proof}
 2695: 
 2696: \begin{corollary}[Odd-layer cover universality]
 2697: \label{cor:odd-rank-pure-universality}
 2698: Let $n\ge3$ be odd and put $k=\omega(n)$. Then $\mathcal E_n=\varnothing$.
 2699: After fixing one prime $\pi_S\in\Pi_\alpha(n_S)$ for every nonempty
 2700: $S\subseteq\mathcal P(n)$, the map
 2701: \[
 2702:  \mathcal C\longmapsto\prod_{S\in\mathcal C}\pi_S
 2703: \]
 2704: embeds $\operatorname{MinCov}(\mathcal P(n))$ into $\mathcal M_n$ and
 2705: preserves the support hypergraph. Hence, if $C_k$ denotes the number of minimal
 2706: covers of a labelled $k$-set, then
 2707: \begin{equation}\label{eq:odd-minimal-cover-bound}
 2708:  \#\mathcal M_n\ge C_k,
 2709:  \qquad
 2710:  \widthop(B_n)\ge C_k.
 2711: \end{equation}
 2712: Through Royle's correspondence
 2713: \cite{Royle2000SetCoversSplitGraphs}, every isomorphism type of split graph on
 2714: $k$ vertices consequently has a rank-pure Fibonacci realization on the prime
 2715: coordinates of $n$.
 2716: \end{corollary}
 2717: 
 2718: \begin{proof}
 2719: Every nonempty $n_S$ is odd and at least $3$, so no support is exceptional.
 2720: Theorem~\ref{thm:rank-pure-universality} gives the embedding and support
 2721: preservation. Minimal generators form an antichain, which proves the width
 2722: bound. Royle's theorem is a correspondence between isomorphism types of split
 2723: graphs and minimal covers; choosing a labelling of a representative cover and
 2724: then applying the embedding gives the final assertion.
 2725: \end{proof}
 2726: 
 2727: We next fix the classical enumeration input, without claiming it as a result of
 2728: this paper. Let $C_{k,s}$ count the minimal covers of a labelled $k$-set having
 2729: $s$ members, put $C_k=\sum_s C_{k,s}$, and put
 2730: \[
 2731:  b_k:=\sum_{s=0}^k\binom{k}{s}2^{s(k-s)}.
 2732: \]
 2733: Hearne and Wagner \cite{HearneWagner1973MinimalCovers} proved
 2734: \begin{equation}\label{eq:hearne-wagner}
 2735:  C_{k,s}=\sum_{j=s}^k\binom{k}{j}
 2736:  \genfrac{\{}{\}}{0pt}{}{j}{s}
 2737:  (2^s-s-1)^{k-j}.
 2738: \end{equation}
 2739: The central-sum asymptotic occurring in the labelled split-graph enumeration of
 2740: Bender--Richmond--Wormald \cite{BenderRichmondWormald1985ChordalSplit}, in the
 2741: form recorded also by Troyka \cite[Section~3]{Troyka2019SplitGraphs}, gives,
 2742: with
 2743: \[
 2744:  \vartheta_0:=\sum_{r\in\mathbb Z}2^{-r^2},\qquad
 2745:  \vartheta_1:=\sum_{r\in\mathbb Z}2^{-(r+1/2)^2},\qquad
 2746:  \varepsilon\equiv k\pmod2,
 2747: \]
 2748: the published asymptotic
 2749: \begin{equation}\label{eq:minimal-cover-theta}
 2750:  C_k\sim b_k\sim
 2751:  \vartheta_\varepsilon\binom{k}{\lfloor k/2\rfloor}2^{k^2/4}.
 2752: \end{equation}
 2753: In particular, $b_{k-1}/b_k=O(2^{-k/2})$.
 2754: 
 2755: \begin{lemma}[Connected minimal-cover concentration]
 2756: \label{lem:connected-minimal-cover-concentration}
 2757: If $C_k^{\rm conn}$ denotes the number of connected minimal covers, then
 2758: \begin{equation}\label{eq:connected-minimal-cover-asymptotic}
 2759:  C_k-C_k^{\rm conn}=O(kC_{k-1})=o(C_k).
 2760: \end{equation}
 2761: \end{lemma}
 2762: 
 2763: \begin{proof}
 2764: Connected components of a minimal cover are themselves minimal covers, so the
 2765: labelled exponential formula gives
 2766: \[
 2767:  C_k=\sum_{j=1}^k\binom{k-1}{j-1}C_j^{\rm conn}C_{k-j}.
 2768: \]
 2769: The terms $j=1$ and $j=k-1$ contribute $O(kC_{k-1})$. Choosing the least
 2770: private element in each cover member gives $C_j\le b_j\le2^j2^{j^2/4}$.
 2771: For the remaining terms, use this bound and the central lower bound for
 2772: $C_{k-1}$ furnished by the $s=\lfloor(k-1)/2\rfloor$ term of
 2773: \eqref{eq:hearne-wagner}. After division by $kC_{k-1}$, the term indexed by
 2774: $j$ is
 2775: \[
 2776:  \ll \binom{k}{j}
 2777:  2^{-(j-1)(k-j-1)/2}\qquad(2\le j\le k-2).
 2778: \]
 2779: The sum of these bounds is bounded (and in fact tends to zero), by symmetry
 2780: and separation into $2\le j\le k/2$ and its reflection. This proves the first
 2781: estimate in \eqref{eq:connected-minimal-cover-asymptotic}; the second equality
 2782: follows from \eqref{eq:minimal-cover-theta}.
 2783: \end{proof}
 2784: 
 2785: \begin{corollary}[Theta-refined connected support entropy]
 2786: \label{cor:theta-refined-connected-entropy}
 2787: Uniformly along every sequence of integers for which $k=\omega(n)\to\infty$,
 2788: \begin{equation}\label{eq:theta-connected-lower-bound}
 2789:  \#\mathcal M_n^{\rm conn}\ge
 2790:  (1-o(1))\,
 2791:  \vartheta_\varepsilon\binom{k}{\lfloor k/2\rfloor}2^{k^2/4},
 2792:  \qquad \varepsilon\equiv k\pmod2.
 2793: \end{equation}
 2794: Consequently
 2795: \begin{equation}\label{eq:theta-connected-log-lower-bound}
 2796:  \log\#\mathcal M_n^{\rm conn}\ge
 2797:  \frac{\log2}{4}k^2+k\log2-\frac12\log k
 2798:  +\log\vartheta_\varepsilon+\frac12\log\frac2\pi+o(1).
 2799: \end{equation}
 2800: The same lower bounds hold with $\mathcal M_n$ in place of
 2801: $\mathcal M_n^{\rm conn}$.
 2802: \end{corollary}
 2803: 
 2804: \begin{proof}
 2805: Fix an exceptional support $S_0\in\mathcal E_n$. If a minimal cover contains
 2806: $S_0$, choose a canonical private vertex $x\in S_0$ of that member. Every
 2807: other member avoids $x$ and is recovered from its canonical private vertex and
 2808: an arbitrary subset of the remaining nonprivate vertices. The number of such
 2809: encodings is at most $b_{k-1}$ for each choice of $x$. Therefore the number of
 2810: minimal covers meeting $\mathcal E_n$ is at most
 2811: \[
 2812:  \sum_{S_0\in\mathcal E_n}|S_0|b_{k-1}\le4b_{k-1}=o(C_k).
 2813: \]
 2814: Lemma~\ref{lem:connected-minimal-cover-concentration} also shows that only
 2815: $o(C_k)$ minimal covers are disconnected. Thus $(1-o(1))C_k$ covers are simultaneously
 2816: connected and disjoint from $\mathcal E_n$. Theorem
 2817: \ref{thm:rank-pure-universality} embeds them in
 2818: $\mathcal M_n^{\rm conn}$, proving \eqref{eq:theta-connected-lower-bound}.
 2819: Taking logarithms and using
 2820: \[
 2821:  \log\binom{k}{\lfloor k/2\rfloor}
 2822:  =k\log2-\frac12\log k+\frac12\log\frac2\pi+o(1)
 2823: \]
 2824: gives \eqref{eq:theta-connected-log-lower-bound}.
 2825: \end{proof}
 2826: 
 2827: \begin{corollary}[Discrete local limit in a fixed-prime rank-pure sector]
 2828: \label{cor:rank-pure-local-limit}
 2829: Let $(n_j)$ be any sequence with $k_j=\omega(n_j)\to\infty$. For every
 2830: $j$ and every nonexceptional nonempty support
 2831: $S\subseteq\mathcal P(n_j)$, fix one prime
 2832: $\pi_{j,S}\in\Pi_\alpha((n_j)_S)$. Let $\mathcal Q_{n_j}^{\rm rp}$ consist of the
 2833: products $\prod_{S\in\mathcal C}\pi_{j,S}$ over all
 2834: $\mathcal C\in\operatorname{MinCov}(\mathcal P(n_j))$ disjoint from
 2835: $\mathcal E_{n_j}$, and choose $m$ uniformly from $\mathcal Q_{n_j}^{\rm rp}$. Put
 2836: $W_j=\omega(m)$. Along the subsequence $k_j=2q_j$, for every fixed
 2837: $d\in\mathbb Z$,
 2838: \begin{equation}\label{eq:even-rank-pure-local-limit}
 2839:  \Pr(W_j=q_j+d)\longrightarrow\frac{2^{-d^2}}{\vartheta_0}.
 2840: \end{equation}
 2841: Along the subsequence $k_j=2q_j+1$,
 2842: \begin{equation}\label{eq:odd-rank-pure-local-limit}
 2843:  \Pr(W_j=q_j+d)\longrightarrow
 2844:  \frac{2^{-(d-1/2)^2}}{\vartheta_1}.
 2845: \end{equation}
 2846: In particular, $W_j=k_j/2+O_{\mathbb P}(1)$.
 2847: \end{corollary}
 2848: 
 2849: \begin{proof}
 2850: Unique factorization and distinct support ranks show that $W_j$ is the number
 2851: of members of the corresponding cover. By the proof of
 2852: Corollary~\ref{cor:theta-refined-connected-entropy}, deleting covers meeting
 2853: $\mathcal E_{n_j}$ changes the uniform distribution in total variation by
 2854: $o(1)$. For $s=\lfloor k/2\rfloor+d$ with fixed $d$, the $j=s$ term of
 2855: \eqref{eq:hearne-wagner} and the canonical-private-element encoding give
 2856: \[
 2857:  \binom{k}{s}(2^s-s-1)^{k-s}
 2858:  \le C_{k,s}\le \binom{k}{s}2^{s(k-s)},
 2859:  \qquad
 2860:  C_{k,s}\sim\binom{k}{s}2^{s(k-s)}.
 2861: \]
 2862: Dividing this by \eqref{eq:minimal-cover-theta} gives
 2863: \eqref{eq:even-rank-pure-local-limit} and
 2864: \eqref{eq:odd-rank-pure-local-limit}. The quadratic limiting tails are
 2865: summable, which is equivalent to tightness on the integer scale.
 2866: \end{proof}
 2867: 
 2868: \begin{remark}\label{rem:connected-lower-not-dominance}
 2869: Corollary~\ref{cor:theta-refined-connected-entropy} proves that connected
 2870: minimal generators alone realize the full currently known lower support
 2871: entropy, including its linear and logarithmic corrections. It does not compare
 2872: the total connected and disconnected parts of $\mathcal M_n$. Such a comparison
 2873: would require upper control of the arithmetic multiplicities in $R(n)$ and is
 2874: not presently available.
 2875: \end{remark}
 2876: 
 2877: \begin{theorem}[Combinatorial support entropy]
 2878: \label{thm:minimal-generator-growth}
 2879: For every integer $n\ge3$, put $k=\omega(n)$ and
 2880: $L(n)=\log\#\mathcal M_n$. Then $\mathcal M_n$ is nonempty. If $k\ge3$ and
 2881: $r=\lfloor k/2\rfloor$, then
 2882: \begin{equation}\label{eq:private-cover-finite-bounds}
 2883:  (2^r-1)^{k-r}
 2884:  \le \#\mathcal M_n
 2885:  \le \sum_{s=1}^{k}\binom{k}{s}(2R(n))^s2^{s(k-s)}.
 2886: \end{equation}
 2887: In particular, uniformly along every sequence for which $\omega(n)\to\infty$,
 2888: \begin{equation}\label{eq:private-cover-entropy-order}
 2889:  \frac{\log2}{4}\,\omega(n)^2-O(1)
 2890:  \le L(n)
 2891:  \le \frac{\log2}{4}\,\omega(n)^2
 2892:       +\omega(n)\log R(n)+O(\omega(n)).
 2893: \end{equation}
 2894: The coefficient $\log2/4$ is the exact entropy coefficient for the support
 2895: combinatorics. This is not an asymptotic formula for $L(n)$ unless
 2896: $R(n)=\exp(o(\omega(n)))$; no such unconditional estimate for $R(n)$ is
 2897: proved here.
 2898: 
 2899: For comparison, the crude support-count estimate gives

codex
The audit has isolated the main risk: statements about the exact coefficient are proved only for the multiplicity-free support-cover contribution, while the fibotomic theorem proves an upper inequality from size and rank-congruence inputs, not an information-theoretic optimum. I will revise those passages to name the precise restricted object and add an explicit open interface requiring both an admissible information class and a realizable extremal model; the mathematical bounds themselves will remain unchanged. Before editing, I am verifying Granville, Hong, and Bugeaud–Luca–Mignotte–Siksek from primary records and full texts, not from the oracle summary.

    <id>http://arxiv.org/abs/1201.6659v1</id>
    <title>Primitive divisors of Lucas and Lehmer sequences</title>
    <updated>2012-01-31T19:49:39Z</updated>
    <link href="https://arxiv.org/abs/1201.6659v1" rel="alternate" type="text/html"/>
    <link href="https://arxiv.org/pdf/1201.6659v1" rel="related" type="application/pdf" title="pdf"/>
    <summary>Stewart reduced the problem of determining all Lucas and Lehmer sequences whose $n$-th element does not have a primitive divisor to solving certain Thue equations. Using the method of Tzanakis and de Weger for solving Thue equations, we determine such sequences for $n \leq 30$. Further computations lead us to conjecture that, for $n &gt; 30$, the $n$-th element of such sequences always has a primitive divisor.</summary>

  <entry>
    <id>http://arxiv.org/abs/1212.6306v1</id>
    <title>Primitive prime factors in second order linear recurrence sequences</title>
    <updated>2012-12-27T05:38:11Z</updated>
    <link href="https://arxiv.org/abs/1212.6306v1" rel="alternate" type="text/html"/>
    <link href="https://arxiv.org/pdf/1212.6306v1" rel="related" type="application/pdf" title="pdf"/>
    <summary>For a class of Lucas sequences ${x_n}$, we show that if $n$ is a positive integer then $x_n$ has a primitive prime factor which divides $x_n$ to an odd power, except perhaps when $n = 1, 2, 3 or 6$. This has several desirable consequences.</summary>
    <category term="math.NT" scheme="http://arxiv.org/schemas/atom"/>
    <published>2012-12-27T05:38:11Z</published>
    <arxiv:comment>To Andrzej Schinzel on his 75th birthday</arxiv:comment>
    <arxiv:primary_category term="math.NT"/>
    <author>
      <name>Andrew Granville</name>
    </author>
  </entry>

> No. 2] Proc. Japan Acad., 81 , Ser. A (2005) 17

## On Fibonacci numbers with few prime divisors 

By Yann Bugeaud ,∗) Florian Luca ,∗∗ ) Maurice Mignotte ,∗) and Samir Siksek ∗∗∗ )  

> (Communicated by Heisuke Hironaka ,m. j. a. , Feb. 14, 2005)

Abstract: If n is a positive integer, write Fn for the nth Fibonacci number, and ω(n) for the number of distinct prime divisors of n. We give a description of Fibonacci numbers satisfying 

ω(Fn) ≤ 2. Moreover, we prove that the inequality ω(Fn) ≥ (log n)log 2+ o(1) holds for almost all 

n. We conjecture that ω(Fn)  log n for composite n, and give a heuristic argument in support of this conjecture. 

Key words: Fibonacci numbers; arithmetic functions; prime divisors. 

1. Introduction. Let Fn be the nth Fi-bonacci number and Ln be the nth Lucas number. In a previous paper [3], the following result was proved. 

Theorem 1. The only solutions to the equa-tion 

Fn = ym, m ≥ 2

are given by n = 0 , 1, 2, 6 and 12 which correspond respectively to Fn = 0 , 1, 1, 8 and 144 . Moreover, the only solutions to the equation 

Ln = yp, m ≥ 2

are given by n = 1 and 3 which correspond respec-tively to Ln = 1 and 4.

The proof involves an intricate combination of Baker’s method and the modular method; it also needs about one week of computer verification us-ing the systems pari [1] and magma [2]. In this paper we use the above theorem, together with well-known results of Carmichael and Cohn to give a descrip-tion of Fibonacci numbers with at most two distinct prime divisors. If n is a positive integer, write ω(n) for the num-ber of distinct prime divisors of n. We prove that the inequality ω(Fn) ≥ (log n)log 2+ o(1) holds for almost all n. We conjecture that ω(Fn)  log n for com-posite n, and give a heuristic argument in support of this conjecture.     

> 2000 Mathematics Subject Classification. Primary 11B39; Secondary 11K65.
> ∗)Universit´ e Louis Pasteur, U. F. R. de math´ ematiques, 7, rue Ren´ e Descartes, 67084 Strasbourg Cedex, France.
> ∗∗ )Instituto de Matem´ aticas, UNAM, Ap. Postal 61-3 (Xangari), C.P. 58089, Morelia, Michoac´ an, Mexico.
> ∗∗∗ )Department of Mathematics and Statistics, College of Science, Sultan Qaboos University, P.O. Box 36, Al-Khod 123, Oman.

2. The theorems of Carmichael and Cohn. We need the following two celebrated re-sults on Fibonacci numbers. The first is due to Carmichael [4] and the second to Cohn [5]. 

Theorem 2. Let n > 2 and n  = 6 , 12 then Fn

has a prime divisor which does not divide any Fm for 

0 < m < n ; such a prime is called a primitive divisor 

of Fn.

Theorem 3. Let 0 < m < n and suppose that the product FmFn is a square, then (m, n ) = (1 , 2) ,

(1 , 12) , (2 , 12) or (3 , 6) .

3. Fibonacci numbers with ω(Fn) ≤ 2. 

Write ω(m) for the number of distinct prime factors of m. Notice that ω(Fn) > 0 for n > 2 and that for integers m and n, with 1 < m < n and mn  = 6, 12, we have (1) ω(Fmn ) > ω (Fm) + ω(Fn), if (m, n ) = 1 ,

because first Fm and Fn both divide Fmn and are coprime when the indices are coprime, and secondly Theorem 2 implies that there is a prime number di-viding Fmn which does not divide the product FmFn.

Lemma 3.1. Suppose n is a positive integer and ω(Fn) ≤ 2. Then either n = 1 , 2, 4, 8, 12 or 

n = , 2,  2 for some odd prime number .Proof. The Lemma follows straightforwardly from the fact that ω(Fn) ≥ 3 in the following cases: (a) if 16 | n, or 24 | n, or (b) if pq | n where p, q are distinct odd primes, or (c) if 3 | n or 2 2 | n or 4  | n for some odd prime 

, unless n = 12. These may be verified using a combination of The-orem 2 and inequality (1). For example, if 4  | n

where   = 3 is an odd prime then 

ω(Fn) ≥ ω(F4) > ω (F2) > ω (F ) ≥ 1,18 Y. Bugeaud et al . [Vol. 81(A), 

where the strict inequalities follow from Theorem 2. Thus ω(Fn) ≥ 3. 

If n = 2  with  > 3 then Fn = FL and 

ω(Fn) = 2 implies that F and L are prime num-bers (because of Theorem 1). Consider now the case n = 2 with ω(Fn) = 2. Then ω(F) = 1 and, by Theorem 1, F = p = prime. If  = 5 then F = 5 and F25 = 5 2 × 3001. If   = 5 then p does not divide Fn/F  so that Fn = pq t, say. By Theorem 3, the product FFn = p2qt is not a square, thus t is odd. We have proved the following result. 

Theorem 4. The only solutions to the equa-tion 

ω(Fn) = 1 

are given by n = 4 , n = 6 or n is an odd prime number for which Fn is prime. The only solutions to the equation 

ω(Fn) = 2 

are given for even n by n = 8 or n = 12 or n = 2 

where  is some odd prime number for which Fn =

FL where F and L are both prime numbers. For odd n, the only possible cases are n =  or n = 2.Moreover if ω(Fn) = 2 and n = 2 then F must be prime, say F = p; and if   = 5 , then 

Fn = pq t

where q is a prime number distinct from p, and the exponent t is odd. 

A short computer search reveals examples of all of the above possibilities. Thus 

• examples of ω(Fn) = 1 with n prime are: F5 =5, F7 = 13, F11 = 89, F13 = 233 , . . . 

• examples of ω(Fn) = 2 with n = 2  are: F10 =55, F14 = 13 × 29, F22 = 89 × 199, F26 = 233 ×

• examples of ω(Fn) = 2 with n = ,

Remark. Lemma 3.1 can also be proved us-ing some results from [7]. For example, one of the results from [7] is that, if τ (m) denotes the number of divisors of the positive integer m, then we have 

τ (Fn) ≥ Fτ(n) for any positive integer n, with equal-ity only for n = 1 , 2, 4. Assuming that ω(Fn) ≤ 2, one gets immediately that (up to a few exceptions) 

τ (n) ≤ 4, giving that n is the product of two dis-tinct prime numbers or the square of a prime number, which together with Theorem 2 yields the conclusion of Lemma 3.1. 

4. Fibonacci numbers rarely have few prime factors. Theorem 4 in effect tells us that 

ω(Fn) ≥ 3 for almost all n. We mean by this that the set of integers for which this inequality holds has density 1. Indeed, much more is true. For exam-ple, by the following theorem, ω(Fn) ≥ C holds for almost all n, whatever the value of C.

Theorem 5. The inequality ω(Fn) ≥

(log n)log 2+ o(1) holds for almost all n.Proof. By Theorem 2, we know that if a divisor 

d of n is not 1, 2, 6 or 12 then Fd has a primitive prime factor. This translates immediately in saying that 

ω(Fn) ≥ τ (n) − 4,

where τ (n) is the total number of divisors of n. Cer-tainly, τ (n) ≥ 2ω(n). Since ω(n) = (1 + o(1)) log log n

for almost all n, the desired inequality follows.

## Abstract

If n$n$ is a positive integer, write F n$F_{n}$ for the n$n$th Fibonacci number, and ω(n)$\omega \left(\right. n \left.\right)$ for the number of distinct prime divisors of n$n$. We give a description of Fibonacci numbers satisfying ω(F n)≤2$\omega \left(\right. F_{n} \left.\right) \leq 2$. Moreover, we prove that the inequality ω(F n)≥(log n)log 2+o(1)$\omega \left(\right. F_{n} \left.\right) \geq \left(\right. log ⁡ n \left.\right)^{log ⁡ 2 + o \left(\right. 1 \left.\right)}$ holds for almost all n$n$. We conjecture that ω(F n)≫log n$\omega \left(\right. F_{n} \left.\right) \gg log ⁡ n$ for composite n$n$, and give a heuristic argument in support of this conjecture.

> arXiv:1212.6306v1 [math.NT] 27 Dec 2012

PRIMITIVE PRIME FACTORS IN SECOND-ORDER LINEAR RECURRENCE SEQUENCES 

Andrew Granville 

To Andrzej Schinzel on his 75th birthday, with thanks for the many inspiring papers 

Abstract. For a class of Lucas sequences {xn}, we show that if n is a positive integer then 

xn has a primitive prime factor which divides xn to an odd power, except perhaps when 

n = 1 , 2, 3 or 6. This has several desirable consequences. 

1. Introduction 

1a. Repunits and primitive prime factors. The numbers 11, 111 and 1111111111 are known as repunits , that is all of their digits are 1 (in base 10). Repunits cannot be squares (since they are ≡ 3 (mod 4)), so one might ask whether a product of distinct repunits can ever be a square? We will prove that this cannot happen. A more interesting example is the set of repunits in base 2, the integers of the form 2 n − 1. In this case there is one easily found product of distinct repunits that is a square, namely (2 3 − 1)(2 6 − 1) = 21 2 (which is 111 · 111111 = 10101 · 10101 in base 2); this turns out to be the only example. For a given sequence of integers {xn}n≥0, we define a characteristic prime factor of 

xn to be a prime p which divides xn but p ∤ xm for 1 ≤ m ≤ n − 1. The Bang-Zsigmondy theorem (1892) states that if r > s ≥ 1 and ( r, s ) = 1 then the numbers 

xn = rn − sn

r − s

have a characteristic prime factor for each n > 1 except for the case 26−1 

> 2−1

. A primitive prime factor of xn is a characteristic prime factor of xn that does not divide r − s.For various Diophantine applications it would be of interest to determine whether there is a characteristic prime factor p of xn for which p2 does not divide xn. As an example 

Many thanks are due to Hendrik Lenstra for informing me of his observation that one can determine the value of the Jacobi symbol ( xm/x n), where xm = 2 m − 1, in terms of the inverse of m mod n. He also proved our results for odd n and then we shared several further email exchanges that led to the results discussed in this paper. I would also like to thank Ilan Vardi for the email discussion alluded to herein, and of course NSERC for partially funding this research.

of such an application, note that if xn1 . . . x nk is a square where 1 < n 1 < n 2 < · · · < n k

and k ≥ 1 then a characteristic prime factor p of xnk divides only xnk in this product and hence must divide xnk to an even power. Thus if p divides xnk to only the first power then xn1 . . . x nk cannot be a square. Unfortunately we are unable to prove anything about characteristic prime factors dividing xn only to the first power, but we are able to show that there is a characteristic prime factor that divides xn to an odd power, which is just as good for this particular application. 

Theorem 1. If r and s are pairwise coprime integers for which 2 divides rs but not 4, then (rn − sn)/(r − s) has a characteristic prime factor which divides it to an odd power, for each n > 1 except perhaps for n = 2 and n = 6 . The case n = 2 is exceptional if and only if r + s is a square. The case n = 6 is exceptional if and only if r2 − rs + s2 is 3 times a square. 

In particular 2 n − 1 has a characteristic prime factor which divides it to an odd power, for all n > 1 except n = 6. Also (10 n − 1) /9 has a characteristic prime factor which divides it to an odd power for all n > 1. One can take these all to be primitive prime factors. 

Corollary 1. Let xn = ( rn − sn)/(r − s) where r and s are pairwise coprime integers for which 2 divides rs but not 4. If xn1 xn2 . . . x nk is a square where 1 < n 1 < n 2 < · · · < n k

and k ≥ 1, then either x2 = r + s is a square, or x3x6 = x23(r3 + s3) is a square.

1b. Certain Lucas sequences. The numbers xn = ( rn −sn)/(r −s) satisfy x0 = 0 , x 1 =1 and the second order linear recurrence xn+2 = ( r + s)xn+1 − rsx n for each n ≥ 0. These are examples of a Lucas sequence, where {xn}n≥0 is a Lucas sequence if x0 = 0 , x 1 = 1 and (1) xn+2 = bx n+1 + cx n for all n ≥ 0,

for given non-zero, coprime integers b, c . The discriminant of the Lucas sequence is ∆ := b2 + 4 c.

Theorem 3. Let b and c be pairwise coprime integers with c ≡ 2 (mod 4) and ∆ =

has a primitive prime factor which (exactly) divides xn to an odd power. 

The exceptions for n = 1 , 2 and 6 are as above in Theorem 2. In fact x3 does not have such a prime factor if and only if x3 = b2 + c equals 3 times a square; 

1c. Fermat’s last theorem and Catalan’s conjecture; and a new observa-tion. Before Wiles’ work, one studied Fermat’s last theorem by considering the equation 

xp + yp = zp for prime exponent p where ( x, y, z ) = 1, and split into two cases depending on whether p divides xyz . In the “first case”, in which p ∤ xyz , one can factor zp − yp

into two coprime factors ( z − y) and ( zp − yp)/(z − y) which must both equal the pth power of an integer. Thus if the pth term of the Lucas sequence xp = ( zp − yp)/(z − y)is never a pth power for odd primes p then the first case of Fermat’s last theorem follows, an approach that has not yet succeeded. However Terjanian [9] did develop these ideas to prove that the first case of Fermat’s last theorem is true for even exponents, showing that if x2p + y2p = z2p in coprime integers x, y, z where p is an odd prime then 2 p divides either 

x or y:In any solution, x or y is even, else 2 divides ( xp)2 + ( yp)2 = z2p but not 4, which is impossible. So we may assume that x is even, but not divisible by p, and y and z are odd so that we have a solution r = z2, s = y2, t = xp to rp − sp = t2 with r ≡ s ≡ 1(mod 4) and ( t, 2p) = 2. Let xn = ( rn − sn)/(r − s) for all n ≥ 1, so that xp(r − s) = t2

and ( xp, r − s) = ( p, r − s)|(p, t ) = 1, which implies that xp is a square. Terjanian’s key observation is that the Jacobi symbols (2)
